The optimal chromatic bound for even-hole-free graphs without induced seven-vertex paths
Shenwei Huang, Yidong Zhou, Yeonsu Chang

Abstract
The class of even-hole-free graphs has been extensively studied on its own and on its relation to perfect graphs. In this paper, we study the χ \chi -boundedness of even-hole-free graphs which itself is an important topic in graph theory. In particular, we prove that every even-hole-free graph G G without induced 7-vertex paths satisfies χ ⁡ ( G ) ≤ ⌈ 5 4 ​ ω ​ ( G ) ⌉ \chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil , where χ ⁡ ( G ) \chi(G) and ω ⁡ ( G ) \omega(G) denote the chromatic number and clique number of G G , respectively. This bound is optimal. Our result strictly extends the result of Karthick and Maffary [ 10 ] on even-hole-free graphs without induced 6-vertex paths, and implies that even-hole-free graphs without induced 7-vertex paths satisfy Reed’s Conjecture. Our proof relies on a heavy structural analysis on a maximal substructure called a nice blowup of a five-cycle and can be viewed for graphs in which all holes are of length five (graphs with all holes having the same length gain increasing interest in recent years [ 6 ] ). Our result gives a partial answer to a conjecture of Wang and Wu [ 22 ] on graphs in which all holes are of length 5. One of the key technical ingredients is a technical lemma proved via clique cutset argument combined with the idea of Infinite Descent Method (often used in number theory).



1
Introduction

For a list of graphs
(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
, we say that a graph
G
is
(H_{1},\ldots,H_{k})
-free
if
G
has no graph
H_{i}
as an induced subgraph for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
\omega(G)
denote the clique number of a graph
G
.
For a positive integer
k
, a
proper
k
-coloring
of a graph
G
is a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}V(G)\rightarrow[k]
such that
f(u)\neq f(v)
for every edge
uv\in E(G)
. In such a case, we say that
G
is
k
-colorable
.
The
chromatic number
of
G
, denoted by
\chi(G)
, is the minimum positive integer
k
such that
G
is
k
-colorable.
For a graph class
\mathcal{G}
, we say that
\mathcal{G}
is
\chi
-bounded
if there exists a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that every graph
G\in\mathcal{G}
has
\chi(G)\leq f(\omega(G))
. The function
f
is called a
\chi
-bounding function
for
\mathcal{G}
.

The study of
\chi
-bounding functions for graph classes is an important area in graph theory in the past two decades, see for example the survey of Scott and Seymour
[
19
]
and Scott’s survey
[
16
]
in Proceedings of International Congress Mathematics 2022. The study can be roughly divided into two lines: graphs with a single forbidden induced subgraph and graphs with forbidden holes. The research on
\chi
-boundedness of graphs with a single forbidden induced subgraph is largely surrounded by Gyárfás-Sumner Conjecture. The conjecture is notoriously hard despite some partial progress (see
[
18
]
for instance).

In this paper, we focus on graphs with forbidden holes.
A
hole
in
G
is an induced cycle of order at least four.
Scott and Seymour
[
17
]
proved that the class of
odd-hole-free
graphs, graphs with no holes of odd length, is
\chi
-bounded with
f=2^{2^{n}}
being a
\chi
-bounding function. It is still open whether this double-exponential bound can be improved to a single-exponential bound. In sharp contrast, the class of
even-hole-free
graphs, graphs with no holes of even length, admits a linear
\chi
-bounding function
f(n)=2n-1
. This follows from a stronger result by Chudnovsky and Seymour
[
4
]
that every even-hole-free graph has a vertex whose neighborhood can be covered by two cliques. It is not known whether this bound is optimal.

In fact, several known results on subclasses of even-hole-free graphs suggest that this bound may not be optimal.

(Pan)

A
pan
is a hole with a pendent edge. It is shown in
[
1
]
that every (pan, even-hole)-free graph
G
has
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{3}{2}\omega(G)\right\rceil
.

(Cap)

A
cap
is a hole with an additional vertex adjacent to an edge on the hole. It is shown in
[
2
]
that every (cap, even-hole)-free graph
G
has
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil
.

(
P_{6}
)

The path on
t
vertices is denoted by
P_{t}
. It is shown in
[
10
]
that every (
P_{6}
, even-hole)-free graph
G
has
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil
.

(Diamond)

A
diamond
is obtained from the complete graph on four vertices by removing an edge. It is shown in
[
11
]
that every (diamond, even-hole)-free graph
G
has
\chi(G)\leq\omega(G)+1
.

For more background and results on even-hole-free graphs, we refer the readers to a comprehensive survey by Vušković
[
21
]
.
The main result of this paper is the following optimal
\chi
-bounding function for the class of even-hole-free graphs without an induced 7-vertex path.

Theorem 1.1
.

For every (
P_{7}
, even-hole)-free graph
G
,
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil
.

Remark.
This bound is optimal as seen by equal-size blowups of a five-cycle (the precise definition of equal-size blowups is given in Section
2
).
Reed’s Conjecture
[
15
]
states that every graph
G
satisfies
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{\Delta(G)+\omega(G)+1}{2}\right\rceil
where
\Delta(G)
is the maximum degree of
G
.
Our result implies by a result in
[
9
]
that the class of (
P_{7}
, even-hole)-free graphs satisfies Reed’s Conjecture.
For an integer
\ell\geq 4
, a graph is
\ell
-holed
if all holes in this graph have length
\ell
.
Such graphs were studied
[
23
]
in the context of algebraic combinatorics and commutative algebra.
A structure theorem for
\ell
-holed graphs
when
\ell\geq 7
is proved in
[
6
]
. Using this structure theorem, Wang and Wu
[
22
]
showed that
f(n)=\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}n\right\rceil
is the optimal
\chi
-bounding function for the class of
\ell
-holed graphs when
\ell\geq 7
is odd. They further conjecture that this bound also holds for
\ell=5
.
Our result is essentially for the class of 5-holed graphs without an induced
P_{7}
, and proves their conjecture for this graph class.
One difficulty in our proof is that there are no structure theorems for 5-holed graphs at hand and we need to do heavy structural analysis along the way.

Proof sketch.
Since the proof is long, let us sketch the proof here. For integer
s\geq 3
, we denote by
C_{s}
the cycle on
s
vertices.
Let
G
be a
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6})
-free (which is the same as (
P_{7}
, even-hole)-free) graph.
The overall strategy is to consider two cases depending on whether
G
contains an induced
C_{7}
. In the case that
G
contains an induced
C_{7}
, we show, based on a structural result of Penev
[
14
]
, that Theorem
1.1
holds (this case is easy).
Afterwards, we can assume that
G
is
C_{7}
-free. In this case, we can assume that
G
contains an induced
C_{5}
(this case is difficult and most of our proofs are devoted to this case) using a classical result on chordal graphs by Dirac (Lemma
2.4
). Then we define and analyze the so-called nice blowup
H
of an induced
C_{5}
that mimics the structure of an induced
C_{5}
. We then partition
V(G)\setminus V(H)
into subsets
A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{5}
in terms of their neighborhoods on
H
.
After proving structural properties of those subsets, we show that Theorem
1.1
holds when
A_{1}\neq\emptyset
(Section
11
),
A_{1}=\emptyset
and
A_{2}\neq\emptyset
(Section
12
), and
A_{1}=A_{2}=\emptyset
but
A_{3}\neq\emptyset
(Section
13
).
These three sections together complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Technical highlights.
To carry out this strategy, we use technical arguments based on several reducible structures in the study of
\chi
-bounding functions-clique cutsets, small vertices (Lemma
2.11
) and
(p,q)
-good subgraphs (Lemma
2.10
). It is easy to show that it suffices to prove Theorem
1.1
for graphs
G
without those structures (Lemma
2.12
).
The first argument is called
clique cutset argument
, which aims to show that the neighborhood of certain subsets is a clique. In some cases, a direct analysis does not work and we need to combine new technical ideas such as nice partitions defined in Section
10
. The idea is similar to Infinite Descent Method in number theory: if the desired property does not hold, one can first show the existence of a nice partition and then argue that a given nice partition can be made “smaller” in the sense defined in Section
10
, as long as
G
has no clique cutsets.
Since the process cannot continue forever,
G
contains a clique cutset, which is a contradiction.
The second argument is called
small vertex argument
.
The idea is to show that the neighborhood of a vertex
v
in
V(G)\setminus K
is a clique where
K
is a clique of
G
.
This implies that
K
is “large”, meaning that
|K|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega(G)}{4}\right\rceil
. Applying this argument to (minimal) simplicial vertices allows us to conclude in many cases that there are four pairwise disjoint “large” cliques whose union is still a clique. This gives a clique of size larger than
\omega(G)
, which is a contradiction. The
good subgraph argument
is to find a
(p,q)
-good subgraph. The
(p,q)
-good subgraphs were already implicit in
[
10
]
. We explicitly formulate this notion and use
(4,5)
-good subgraphs to prove our main theorem.
This argument is technically involved and used in Sections
3
and
13
. The final argument is called
pre-coloring argument
. For graphs
G
that cannot be handled by the previous three arguments, we need to give an explicit proper coloring using
\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil
colors. The idea is to color a subgraph of
G
first and then color the rest of the graph by reducing the problem (based on structural insight) to color hyperholes (Lemma
2.1
). To be able to apply Lemma
2.1
, we need to carefully choose the subgraph to be pre-colored so that the calculations can go through.

Paper organization.
The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we define terminology and notations used in this paper and prove some general lemmas. In Section
3
, we handle the case that
G
contains an induced
C_{7}
. In Section
4
, we define nice blowups of
C_{5}
and their attachments
A_{0},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{5}
and prove some basic properties about those sets.
In Section
5
, we show that
A_{0}=\emptyset
. In Sections
6
-
8
, we obtain further properties of
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
. In Section
9
, we prove a useful lemma about components of non-neighbors of a
C_{5}
. In Section
10
, we prove a key technical lemma. In Section
11
, we prove Theorem
1.1
when
A_{1}
is not empty. In Section
12
, we prove Theorem
1.1
when
A_{1}
is empty and
A_{2}
is not empty. In Section
13
, we prove Theorem
1.1
when
A_{1},A_{2}
are empty and
A_{3}
is not empty. In Section
14
, we give some concluding remarks.

2
Preliminaries

All graphs in this paper are simple and finite.
Let
G
be a graph. We denote by
V(G)
and
E(G)
the
vertex set
and the
edge set
of
G
, respectively. For an edge
\{u,v\}
of
G
, we shortly say
uv
. For
S\subseteq V(G)
, let
G[S]
denote the subgraph of
G

induced by

S
, and let
G-S=G[V(G)\setminus S]
. For convenience, we identify a subset
S\subseteq V(G)
as
G[S]
.
A graph
H
is an
induced subgraph
of
G
if there exists a subset
S\subseteq V(G)
such that
G[S]
is isomorphic to
H
.
For two disjoint vertex sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
in
G
,
S_{1}
is
complete
to
S_{2}
if every vertex in
S_{1}
is adjacent to every vertex in
S_{2}
, and
S_{1}
is
anticomplete
to
S_{2}
if there are no edges between
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
. If
S_{1}=\{v\}
, we simply say that
v
is complete or anticomplete to
S_{2}
instead of
\{v\}
is complete or anticomplete to
S_{2}
. A vertex
v

mixes on
a subset
S
if
v
is neither complete nor anticomplete to
S
. We say
v
mixes on an edge
xy
if
v
mixes on
\{x,y\}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of
G
and
H
be a subgraph of
V(G)
. The neighbors of
v
in
G
that are contained in
H
is denoted by
N_{H}(v)
. So
N_{G}(v)
is the set of all neighbors of
v
in
G
. The
degree
of
v
in
G
, denoted by
d_{G}(v)
, is the number of neighbors of
v
in
G
.
Let
N_{G}[v]=N_{G}(v)\cup\{v\}
.
For a non-empty vertex subset
S
of
G
, we define
N_{H}(S)=\left(\bigcup_{v\in S}N_{H}(v)\right)\setminus S
.
If the context is clear, we shall omit the subscript
G
, writing
N_{G}(v)
as
N(v)
and so on. We say that
v\in V(H)
is
universal
in
H
if
N_{H}[v]=V(H)
. A set
S\subseteq V(H)
is universal in
H
if each vertex in
S
is universal in
H
.

For a positive integer
n
, let
[n]
denote the set of positive integers at most
n
. For two integers
i,j\in[n]
, the operations
+
and
-
are the same as in the finite cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
For a positive integer
n
, let
P_{n}
denote the path on
n
vertices where
V(P_{n})=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and
E(P_{n})=\{v_{i}v_{i+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in[n-1]\}
. The
internal
of
P
is
V(P)\setminus\{v_{1},v_{n}\}
.
Let
C_{n}
denote the cycle on
n
vertices where
V(C_{n})=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and
E(C_{n})=\{v_{i}v_{i+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}i\in[n]\}
.
For clarity, we write
P_{n}
as
v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{n}
and write
C_{n}
as
v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{n}-v_{1}
. For
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
in
P_{n}
with
i<j
, we denote by
v_{i}-P-v_{j}
the
subpath
of
P
from
v_{i}
to
v_{j}
.
We say that a graph
G
is
connected
if there is a path between
v
to
w
for every vertices
v,w\in V(G)
.
A
component
of
G
is a maximal connected subgraph of
G
.
The complete graph of order
n
is denoted by
K_{n}
.
The
clique number
of a graph
G
, denoted by
\omega(G)
, is the size of a largest clique in
G
. The
independence number
of a graph
G
, denoted by
\alpha(G)
, is the size of a largest stable set in
G
.
A vertex subset
S
of a connected graph
G
is said to be a
clique cutset
if
S
is a clique and
G-S
is not connected. If
H_{1},\ldots,H_{t}
are components of
G-S
where
S
is a clique cutset, then
G[V(H_{i})\cup S]
(
i\in[t]
) is called an
S
-component
. When
G
is disconnected, any component of
G
is simply an
S
-component for
S=\emptyset
.

A graph
H
is said to be a
blowup
of a graph
G
if
H
is obtained from
G
by replacing each vertex
v
of
G
by some (possibly empty) clique
C_{v}
and adding all edges between
C_{v}
and
C_{w}
when
vw
is an edge of
G
. If
C_{v}=\emptyset
for some
v
, it means to remove
v
from
G
. If
C_{v}\neq\emptyset
for each
v\in V(G)
, then
H
is called a
non-empty
blowup of
G
.
If
C_{v}
is of the same size for all
v
, then
H
is called an
equal-size blowup
of
G
.
For an integer
k\geq 4
, a
k
-hyperhole
is a non-empty blowup of
C_{k}=v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{k}-v_{1}
. We denote
C_{k}[s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}]
by a
k
-hyperhole where the clique
C_{v_{i}}
substituting for
v_{i}
has size
s_{i}
.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
13
]
)
.

For any
k
-hyperhole
H
,
\chi(H)=\max\left\{\omega(H),\left\lceil\frac{|V(H)|}{\alpha(H)}\right\rceil\right\}
.

We first present two simple properties of
C_{4}
-free graphs.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
G
be a
C_{4}
-free graph. For any clique
K
of
G
and any edge
vw\in E(G)
with
v,w\notin K
,
N_{K}(v)\subseteq N_{K}(w)
or
N_{K}(w)\subseteq N_{K}(v)
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Then there exist vertices
a\in N_{K}(v)\setminus N_{K}(w)
and
b\in N_{K}(w)\setminus N_{K}(v)
. It follows that
a-v-w-b-a
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
G
be a
C_{4}
-free graph and
a-b-c
be an induced
P_{3}
in
G
. If
c
has a neighbor
d
not adjacent to
b
, then
d
is not adjacent to
a
.

Proof.

If not, then
a-b-c-d-a
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

Throughout the paper, we will use Lemmas
2.2
and
2.3
without referring to them.
A graph is
chordal
if it has no induced cycle of length at least 4. A vertex
v
in a graph
G
is
simplicial in

G
if
N_{G}[v]
is a clique. In this case, we also say that
v
is a simplicial vertex of
G
.
The following is a well-known result for chordal graphs.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
8
]
)
.

For every chordal graph
G
that is not a complete graph, there are two non-adjacent simplicial vertices of
G
.

Lemma
2.4
implies that every chordal graph
G
has
\chi(G)=\omega(G)
. Next, we define the notion of minimal simplicial vertices.

Definition 2.5

(Minimal simplicial vertices)
.

Let
G
be a graph, and
X
and
Y
be two disjoint subsets of
V(G)
.
For a simplicial vertex
s^{\prime}
of
X
, we say that
s
is a
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex arising from
s^{\prime}
if
s\in N_{X}[s^{\prime}]
is simplicial in
X
and
N_{Y}(s)
is minimal (under set inclusion) over all vertices in
N_{X}[s^{\prime}]
simplicial in
X
. We say
s
is
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex if it is a
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex arising from some simplicial vertex of
X
.

The next lemma says that two non-adjacent simplicial vertices give rise to two non-adjacent minimal simplicial vertices.

Lemma 2.6

(Two non-adjacent minimal simplicail vertices)
.

If
s_{j}
is a
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex arising from a simplicial vertex
s^{\prime}_{j}
of
X
for
j=1,2
with
s^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{2}\notin E(G)
, then
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
and
s_{1}s_{2}\notin E(G)
.

Proof.

Note that
s_{1}
may be
s^{\prime}_{1}
and
s_{2}
may be
s^{\prime}_{2}
. If
s_{1}=s^{\prime}_{1}
and
s_{2}=s^{\prime}_{2}
, then we are done. So we may assume that
s_{1}\neq s^{\prime}_{1}
. As
s^{\prime}_{1}
mixes on
\{s_{1},s^{\prime}_{2}\}
,
s_{1}\neq s^{\prime}_{2}
. Since
s_{1}
is simplicial in
X
and
s^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{2}\notin E(G)
,
s_{1}
is not adjacent to
s^{\prime}_{2}
and thus
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
. If
s_{2}=s^{\prime}_{2}
, we are done. So
s_{2}\neq s^{\prime}_{2}
. If
s_{1}s_{2}\in E(G)
, then
s^{\prime}_{1},s_{2}
are two neighbors of
s_{2}
but they are not adjacent. This contradicts that
s_{2}
is a simplicial vertex of
X
.
∎

The next lemma is a useful property of minimal simplicial vertex which will be used frequently.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
G
be a
C_{4}
-free graph and let
X,Y\subseteq V(G)
be disjoint subsets of
V(G)
with
Y
being a clique. For any
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex
s
,
N_{X\cup Y}(s)
is a clique or there is an induced
P_{4}=a-s-c-d
with
a\in Y
and
c,d\in X
.

Proof.

Suppose that
s
is a
(X,Y)
-minimal simplicial vertex arising from
s^{\prime}\in X
.
Suppose that
N_{X\cup Y}(s)
is not clique.
Let
v,v^{\prime}\in N_{X\cup Y}(s)
be non-adjacent.
Since
s
is simplicial in
X
and
Y
is a clique, we may assume that
v\in X
and
v^{\prime}\in Y
.
Suppose first that
v
is simplicial in
X
.
Then
v\in N_{X}[v]=N_{X}[s]=N_{X}[s^{\prime}]
. By the choice of
s
,
v
has a neighbor
w\in Y
which is not adjacent to
s
.
Then
v^{\prime}-s-v-w-v^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
v
is not simplicial in
X
and thus has a neighbor
w\in X\setminus N[s]
.
Now
v^{\prime}-s-v-w
is a desired induced
P_{4}
.
∎

The next lemma allows to conclude that certain subgraphs are
P_{4}
-free.

Lemma 2.8

(The
P_{4}
-free lemma)
.

Let
G
be
(C_{4},C_{6})
-free graph and
u\in V(G)
. If
X
and
Y
are disjoint subsets of
V(G)\setminus\{u\}
satisfies

\bullet

u
is complete to
X
and anticomplete to
Y
,

\bullet

every two non-adjacent vertices in
X
have their non-empty neighborhoods in
Y
complete to each other, and

\bullet

there is no induced
P_{5}=a-b-c-d-e
with
e\in Y
and
a,b,c,d\in X
,

then
X
is
P_{4}
-free.

Proof.

Suppose that
a-b-c-d
is an induced
P_{4}
in
X
. Let
a^{\prime}\in X
be a neighbor of
a
and
d^{\prime}\in X
be a neighbor of
d
. Then
a^{\prime}d^{\prime}\in E(G)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
a^{\prime}c,a^{\prime}d,d^{\prime}a,d^{\prime}b\notin E(G)
. If
a^{\prime}b,d^{\prime}c\in E(G)
, then
a^{\prime}-b-c-d^{\prime}-a^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
a^{\prime}b,d^{\prime}c\notin E(G)
, then
a-b-c-d-d^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-a
is an induced
C_{6}
. So we may assume that
a^{\prime}b\in E(G)
and
d^{\prime}c\notin E(G)
. Then
a-b-c-d-d^{\prime}
is an induced
P_{5}
violating the last condition of
X
and
Y
.
∎

Next we define certain special subgraphs that are useful for induction.

Definition 2.9

(
(p,q)
-good subgraphs)
.

For two positive integers
p
and
q
, an induced subgraph
H
of a graph
G
is said to be a
(p,q)
-good subgraph
if

(1)

for every maximal clique
K
of
G
,
K
contains at least
p-(\omega(G)-\mathinner{\!\left\lvert K\right\rvert})
vertices from
H
and

(2)

H
is
q
-colorable.

Note that a
(1,1)
-good subgraph is a stable set whose removal decreases the clique number by at least one. This kind of stable set is called
good stable set
in the literature. In particular, a universal vertex is a good stable set and so is a
(1,1)
-good subgraph.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
p
and
q
be positive integers.
If a graph
G
contains a
(p,q)
-good subgraph
H
and
\chi(G-V(H))\leq\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\omega(G-V(H))\right\rceil
, then
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\omega(G)\right\rceil
.

Proof.

We first show that
\omega(G-V(H))\leq\omega(G)-p
.
Let
K^{*}
be a maximum clique in
G-V(H)
and
K
be a maximal clique of
G
containing
K^{*}
. Since
H
is
(p,q)
-good,
|K\cap H|\geq p-(\omega(G)-|K|)
. So

\displaystyle|K^{*}|

\displaystyle=|K|-|K\cap H|\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(K^{*}\text{ is a maximum clique in }G-V(H))

\displaystyle\leq|K|-(p-(\omega(G)-|K|))

\displaystyle=\omega(G)-p.

It follows that

\displaystyle\chi(G)

\displaystyle\leq\chi(G-V(H))+\chi(H)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\omega(G-V(H))\right\rceil+q\quad\quad(\text{assumption})

\displaystyle\leq\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}(\omega(G)-p)\right\rceil+q\quad\quad\quad(\omega(G-V(H))\leq\omega(G)-p))

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{q}{p}\omega(G)\right\rceil.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.11

(Reducible structures)
.

Let
G
be a connected graph and
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a function with
f(n+1)\geq f(n)+1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the following holds.

(1)

If
G
has a universal vertex
u
and
\chi(G-u)\leq f(\omega(G-u))
, then
\chi(G)\leq f(\omega(G))
.

(2)

If
G
has a clique cutset
K
with
G_{1},\ldots,G_{t}
being
K
-components such that
\chi(G_{i})\leq f(\omega(G_{i}))
for
i\in[t]
, then
\chi(G)\leq f(\omega(G))
.

(3)

If
G
has a vertex
u
with
d(u)\leq f(\omega(G))-1
and
\chi(G-u)\leq f(\omega(G-u))
, then
\chi(G)\leq f(\omega(G))
.

Proof.

We prove one by one.

(1) It is clear that
\chi(G)\leq\chi(G-u)+1
and
\omega(G-u)=\omega(G)-1
. So

\displaystyle\chi(G)

\displaystyle\leq f(\omega(G-u))+1

\displaystyle=f(\omega(G)-1)+1

\displaystyle\leq(f(\omega(G))-1)+1\quad\quad\quad(f(n+1)\geq f(n)+1)

\displaystyle=f(\omega(G)).

(2) It is known
[
20
]
that
\chi(G)=\max\limits_{i\in[t]}\chi(G_{i})
. So

\displaystyle\chi(G)

\displaystyle=\max_{i\in[t]}\chi(G_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i\in[t]}f(\omega(G_{i}))

\displaystyle\leq f(\omega(G)).\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad(f(n+1)\geq f(n)+1)

(3) It follows that
\chi(G-u)\leq f(\omega(G-u))\leq f(\omega(G))
since
f
is increasing. So
G-u
has a
f(\omega(G))
-coloring.
Since
u
has degree at most
f(\omega(G))-1
,
N(u)
are colored with at most
f(\omega(G))-1
colors. So we can assign a color from
[f(\omega(G))]
to
u
. This shows that
\chi(G)\leq f(\omega(G))
.
∎

Let
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a function with
f(n+1)\geq f(n)+1
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A vertex
v
of
G
is said to be
f
-small
if
d_{G}(v)\leq f(\omega(G))-1
. We say
v
is
small
if it is
f
-small for
f(n)=\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}n\right\rceil
.
In particular, any simplicial vertex is small.
We say that a graph
G
is
basic
if it is connected and has no
(4,5)
-good subgraphs, no
(1,1)
-good subgraphs, no clique cutsets and no small vertices. The next lemma follows immediately from Lemmas
2.10
and
2.11
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph class. If every basic graph
G^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
has
\chi(G^{\prime})\leq\left\lceil\frac{5\omega(G^{\prime})}{4}\right\rceil
, then every graph
G
in
\mathcal{G}
has
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5\omega(G)}{4}\right\rceil
.

In the following, we can assume that
G
is basic unless otherwise stated. For convenience, we set
\omega=\omega(G)
.

3
Containing an induced
C_{7}

In this section, we assume that a
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6})
-free graph
G
contains an induced
C=C_{7}=v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{7}-v_{1}
.
Our main goal of this section is to show that
\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(G)\right\rceil
for such a graph
G
.
We need to use a known result of Penev
[
14
]
.
To introduce the result, we first define three graphs
M
,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. The graph
M
has
V(M)=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{12}\}
and
E(M)
as follows.

•

v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{7}-v_{1}
is an induced
C_{7}
.

•

\{v_{8},v_{9},v_{10},v_{11},v_{12}\}
induces a clique.

•

v_{8}
is complete to
\{v_{6},v_{7},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{4},v_{5}\}
.

•

v_{9}
is complete to
\{v_{7},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{5},v_{6}\}
.

•

v_{10}
is complete to
\{v_{3},v_{4},v_{5},v_{6},v_{7}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
.

•

v_{11}
is complete to
\{v_{3},v_{6},v_{7}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{4},v_{5}\}
.

•

v_{12}
is complete to
\{v_{3},v_{4},v_{7}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{5},v_{6}\}
.

The graph
M_{1}
has
V(M_{1})=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{9},v^{*}\}
and
E(M_{1})
as follows.

•

v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{7}-v_{1}
is an induced
C_{7}
.

•

v_{8}-v_{9}-v^{*}
is an induced
P_{3}
.

•

v_{8}
is complete to
\{v_{6},v_{7},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{4},v_{5}\}
.

•

v_{9}
is complete to
\{v_{7},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{5},v_{6}\}
.

•

v^{*}
is complete to
\{v_{1},v_{4},v_{5}\}
and anticomplete to
\{v_{2},v_{3},v_{6},v_{7}\}
.

The graph
M_{2}=M_{1}-v_{9}
(see Figure
1
for
M,M_{1},M_{2}
).
A blowup of
M
is
special
if the cliques substituting
v_{i}\in V(M)
is not empty for each
i\in[7]
.

(Theorem 3.11 in
[

]
)
.

G
is a special blowup of

or a non-empty blowup of

or

.

.

:

.

We first deal with special blowups of
M
.
Let
G=L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{12}
be a blowup of
M
where
L_{i}
is the clique substituting
v_{i}
for
i\in[12]
with
L_{j}\neq\emptyset
for each
j\in[7]
.
By Lemma
2.1
, we may assume that
L_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
j\in\{8,9,10,11,12\}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For each
i\in[7]
,
|L_{i}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
and
|L_{i}|\geq 3
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to prove the lemma for
i=2,3,4,5
.

(L_{2})

Let
v\in L_{1}
. Then
N(v)\setminus L_{2}\subseteq L_{1}\cup L_{7}\cup L_{8}\cup L_{9}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|L_{2}|\geq|N(v)\cap L_{2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

(L_{3})

Let
v\in L_{2}
. Then
N(v)\setminus L_{3}\subseteq L_{1}\cup L_{8}\cup L_{9}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|L_{3}|\geq|N(v)\cap L_{3}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

(L_{4})

Let
v\in L_{5}
. Then
N(v)\setminus L_{4}\subseteq L_{6}\cup L_{10}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|L_{4}|\geq|N(v)\cap L_{4}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

(L_{5})

Let
v\in L_{4}
. Then
N(v)\setminus L_{5}\subseteq L_{3}\cup L_{9}\cup L_{10}\cup L_{12}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|L_{5}|\geq|N(v)\cap L_{5}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil

Since
\omega\geq 2
,
|L_{i}|\geq 2
for
i\in[7]
. Since
L_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
j\in\{8,9,10,11,12\}
,
\omega\geq 5
and so
|L_{i}|\geq 3
for
i\in[7]
.
∎

We show that
G
is not basic by finding a
(4,5)
-good subgraph, which contradicts our assumption on
G
. By Lemma
3.2
, one can choose three vertices from each
L_{i}
for
i\in[7]
when constructing
(4,5)
-good subgraphs. We first give two lemmas (Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
) and then consider all possible cases depending on the number of non-empty
L_{j}
s for
j\in\{8,9,10\}
(Lemmas
3.5
-
3.8
).

Lemma 3.3
.

If
L_{10}=L_{11}=L_{12}=\emptyset
, then
G
has a
(4,5)
-good subgraph.

Proof.

Suppose not. Choose two vertices in
L_{i}
for each
i\in[7]
and let
H
be the union of all those chosen vertices. By Lemma
2.1
,
\chi(H)\leq 5
.
It is easy to see that every maximal clique of
G
contains 4 vertices of
H
. Therefore,
H
is a
(4,5)
-good subgraph. This contradicts that
G
is basic.
∎

.

If
L_{10}=\emptyset
and

has a

-good subgraph.

-good subgraph with a 5-coloring as follows (see Figure

1,3,5
L_{5}
4
L_{6}
1,2,3
L_{7}
5
L_{12}

(f)

A
(4,5)
-good subgraph in Lemma
3.8
.

Figure 4
:
(4,5)
-good subgraphs in Lemmas
3.6
-
3.8
.

4
Nice blowups of
C_{5}

From now on, we may assume that our graph
G
is
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6},C_{7})
-free. If
G
is
C_{5}
-free, then
G
is chordal and so
\chi(G)=\omega(G)
. So we assume that
G
contains an induced
C_{5}
.
The indices below are modulo 5.

A tuple
(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4},B_{5})
is called a
nice blowup of
C_{5}
in a graph
G
if

•

each
B_{i}
is a clique in
G
, and distinct
B_{i}
and
B_{j}
are disjoint,

•

for every
i\in[5]
and
v\in B_{i}
,
v
has a neighbor in
B_{i-1}
and a neighbor in
B_{i+1}
,

•

for every
i\in[5]
,
B_{i}
is anticomplete to
B_{i+2}
,

•

for every
i\in[5]
,
a\in B_{i}
,
b,c\in B_{i+1}
,
d\in B_{i+2}
with
b\neq c
,
G[\{a,b,c,d\}]
is not isomorphic to
P_{4}
.

We call the last condition of nice blowups the
P_{4}
-structure
condition. Observe that a nice blowup of
C_{5}
is
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6})
-free. Next we give some basic properties of nice blowups of
C_{5}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
G
be a
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6})
-free graph, and
let
H=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4},B_{5})
be a nice blowup of
C_{5}
in
G
.

(1)

One can order vertices in
B_{i}
as
b_{i}^{1},b_{i}^{2},\ldots,b_{i}^{|B_{i}|}
so that
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(b_{i}^{1})\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(b_{i}^{2})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(b_{i}^{|B_{i}|})
.

(2)

For each
i\in[5]
, there is a vertex in
B_{i}
complete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
. In particular, each vertex in
B_{i-1}
and each vertex in
B_{i+1}
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
.

Proof.

We prove the properties one by one.

(1) It suffices to show that for every two distinct vertices
x,y\in B_{i}
, we have
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)
or
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)
. By Lemma
2.2
, we may assume that
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
. If
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)=N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
, we are done by Lemma
2.2
. So we may assume that exists a vertex
z\in B_{i-1}
that is adjacent to
y
but not adjacent to
x
. Then the
P_{4}
-structure condition implies that
N_{B_{i+1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i+1}}(y)
. So
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)
.

(2) If
b_{i}^{|B_{i}|}
is not adjacent to a vertex
v\in B_{j}
for some
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
, then
v
is anticomplete to
B_{i}
by (2). This contradicts the definition of nice blowup of
C_{5}
. So
b_{i}^{|B_{i}|}
is a desired vertex.
∎

Let
H=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4},B_{5})
be a nice blowup of
C_{5}
in a graph
G
. For each
i\in[5]
, we fix a vertex
q_{i}\in B_{i}
that is complete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
. We point out that nice blowups of
C_{5}
coincide with
5-rings
defined in
[
12
]
.
It was proved in
[
12
]
(Lemma 6.7)
that any nice blowup
H
of
C_{5}
has
\chi(H)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega(H)\right\rceil
. So we can assume that
V(G)\setminus V(H)
is not empty.

For every vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus V(H)
, we write
\operatorname{supp}(v)=\{i\in[5]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}B_{i}\cap N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset\}
.
Next, we investigate how the vertices outside
H
attach to
H
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For
v\in V(G)\setminus V(H)
,
\operatorname{supp}(v)
consists of consecutive integers modulo
5
that has size in
\{0,1,2,3,5\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\operatorname{supp}(v)
does not consist of consecutive integers modulo
5
.
Then there exists
i\in[5]
where
N_{B_{i}}(v)
and
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
are non-empty and
N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
is empty. Let
u\in N_{B_{i}}(v)
and
w\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Then
v-u-q_{i+1}-w-v
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction. By the same argument,
\operatorname{supp}(v)
cannot have size
4
.
∎

Definition 4.3

(Attachment of
H
)
.

We divide the vertices in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
as follows. Let
i\in[5]
.

•

A_{0}
is the set of vertices
v
in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
where
\operatorname{supp}(v)=\emptyset
.

•

A_{1}(i)
is the set of vertices
v
in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
where
\operatorname{supp}(v)=\{i\}
.

•

A_{2}(i)
is the set of vertices
v
in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
where
\operatorname{supp}(v)=\{i,i+1\}
.

•

A_{3}(i)
is the set of vertices
v
in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
where
\operatorname{supp}(v)=\{i-1,i,i+1\}
.

•

A_{5}
is the set of vertices
v
in
V(G)\setminus V(H)
where
\operatorname{supp}(v)=[5]
.

For each
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
A_{j}=\bigcup_{i\in[5]}A_{j}(i)
.

By Lemma
4.2
, the union of above sets is
V(G)\setminus V(H)
. For convenience, we write
A_{x}(i\pm y)
for the union of
A_{x}(i+y)
and
A_{x}(i-y)
.
Let
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)=A_{3}(i)\cup B_{i}
for
i\in[5]
.

Let
G
be a graph and
P=v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{7}
be a 7-vertex path (not necessarily induced) in
G
. We say that
P
is
bad
if
v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{6}
is an induced
P_{6}
and
v_{3}-v_{4}-\cdots-v_{7}
is an induced
P_{5}
. The following observation encodes
(P_{7},C_{6},C_{7})
-freeness by bad
P_{7}
s.

Lemma 4.4
.

A
(P_{7},C_{6},C_{7})
-free graph does not have a bad
P_{7}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
P=v_{1}-v_{2}-\cdots-v_{7}
is a bad
P_{7}
. If
v_{7}v_{2}\in E(G)
, then
v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{6}-v_{7}-v_{2}
is an induced
C_{6}
. So we may assume that
v_{7}v_{2}\notin E(G)
. Now
V(P)
induces either a
C_{7}
or a
P_{7}
depending on whether
v_{7}v_{1}\in E(G)
.
∎

Next we define the notions of induced sequences and induced cyclic sequences which will be useful for finding forbidden induced paths and cycles.

Definition 4.5

(Induced sequences and induced cyclic sequences)
.

Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
be disjoint sets of vertices in
G
where
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that a sequence
A_{1}-A_{2}-\cdots-A_{m}
is an
induced sequence
if

•

for all
i\in[m]
,
A_{i}
is connected,

•

for all
i\in[m-1]
, there is an edge between
A_{i}
and
A_{i+1}
, and

•

for
i,j\in[m]
with
i<j
, there is no edge between
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
.

Similarly, a sequence
A_{1}-A_{2}-\cdots-A_{m}-A_{1}
is an
induced cyclic sequence
if

•

for all
i\in[m]
,
A_{i}
is connected,

•

for all
i\in[m-1]
, there is an edge between
A_{i}
and
A_{i+1}
, and

•

for
i,j\in[m]
with
|j-i|\not\equiv 0,1\pmod{m}
, there is no edge between
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
.

If
A_{i}
is a singleton
\{v\}
, then we may replace
A_{i}
with
v
in the notation. So induced sequences and induced cyclic sequences generalize induced paths and induced cycles, respectively.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
A_{1}-A_{2}-\cdots-A_{m}
is an induced sequence in
G
, then
G
contains an induced path with at least
m
vertices between any vertex in
A_{1}
and any vertex in
A_{m}
.

Proof.

Note that
A=\bigcup_{i\in[m]}A_{i}
is connected by the first two conditions of induced sequences.
Let
P\subseteq A
be a shortest path between a vertex in
A_{1}
and a vertex in
A_{m}
.
By the third condition of induced sequences,

P
contains at least one vertex in each
A_{i}
and so
P
is a desired path.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

If
A_{1}-A_{2}-\cdots-A_{m}-A_{1}
is an induced cyclic sequence in
G
with
m\geq 3
, then
G
contains an induced cycle with at least
m
vertices.

Proof.

Let
v_{j}\in A_{j}
be a vertex having a neighbor in
A_{m}
for
j\in\{1,m-1\}
.
By Lemma
4.6
, there exists an induced path
P
contained in
A_{1}\cup\cdots\cup A_{m-1}
between
v_{1}
and
v_{m-1}
with at least
m-1
vertices.
Let
v^{\prime}_{1}\in P
be the last vertex (from
v_{1}
to
v_{m-1}
) in
A_{1}
having a neighbor in
A_{m}
and
v^{\prime}_{m-1}\in P
be the last vertex (from
v_{m-1}
to
v_{1}
) in
A_{m-1}
having a neighbor in
A_{m}
.
Let
Q
be a shortest path between
v^{\prime}_{1}
and
v^{\prime}_{m-1}
whose internal is contained in
A_{m}
.
Then
v^{\prime}_{1}-P-v^{\prime}_{m-1}-Q-v^{\prime}_{1}
is a desired induced cycle.
∎

4.1
Basic properties

In this subsection, we prove some basic properties that are used throughout the paper.

Lemma 4.8
.

For a connected subgraph
K
of
A_{2}(i)
,
N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(i)}(K)
is complete to
N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)}(K)
.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that there exist
v\in N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(i)}(K)
and
w\in N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)}(K)
that are not adjacent. Then
v-K-w-N_{B_{i+2}}(w)-q_{i-2}-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-v
is an induced cyclic sequence, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
u\in A_{1}(i)
and
v\in A_{2}(i+2)
such that
uv\in E(G)
.
Then
v
is complete to
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i-2}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
v
has a non-neighbor
a
in
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i-2}
. By symmetry, we may assume that
a\in B_{i-2}
.
Let
b\in N_{B_{i}}(u)
.
If
a
has a neighbor
x
in
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
, then
v-u-b-q_{i-1}-a-x-v
is an induced
C_{6}
. So assume that
a
has no neighbor in
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Let
y\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
z\in N_{B_{i+2}}(a)
. Then
v-u-b-q_{i-1}-a-z-y-v
is an induced
C_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

A nice blowup
H
of
C_{5}
is
maximal
if there is no nice blowup
H^{\prime}
of
C_{5}
such that
V(H)\subset V(H^{\prime})
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
H=(B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4},B_{5})
be a maximal nice blowup of
C_{5}
and
v\in A_{3}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
. The following properties hold.

(1)

B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
.

(2)

Either
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
or
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
.

(3)

B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(v)
is anticomplete to either
N_{B_{i-1}}(v)
or
N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
.

Proof.

We prove the properties one by one.

(1) Suppose for contradiction that
N_{B_{i}}(v)=B_{i}
.
For every
j\neq i
, let
B_{j}^{\prime}=B_{j}
and let
B_{i}^{\prime}=B_{i}\cup\{v\}
.
We claim that
(B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime},B_{3}^{\prime},B_{4}^{\prime},B_{5}^{\prime})
is also a nice blowup of
C_{5}
, which leads a contradiction to the assumption that
H
is maximal.

The first three conditions of a nice blowup are straightforward to verify.
Suppose that for some
p\in[5]
, there exist four vertices
a\in B_{p}^{\prime}
,
b,c\in B_{p+1}^{\prime}
,
d\in B_{p+2}^{\prime}
with
b\neq c
where
G[\{a,b,c,d\}]
is isomorphic to
P_{4}
.
As
d
has a neighbor in
e\in B_{p-2}^{\prime}
, it follows that
a-b-c-d-e-q_{i-1}-a
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction.

(2) Suppose that
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)=\emptyset
and
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)=\emptyset
. By (1), there is a vertex in
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(v)
, say
u
. By definition,
u
has a neighbor
w
in
B_{i-1}
and a neighbor
z
in
B_{i+1}
. Then
v-w-u-z-v
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction.

(3) Suppose that there is an edge between
a\in B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(v)
and
b\in N_{B_{i-1}}(v)
, and there is an edge between
c\in B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(v)
and
d\in N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
.
If
a=c
, then
a-b-v-d-a
is an induced
C_{4}
. So we may assume that
a\neq c
. This implies that
c
is not adjacent to
b
, and
a
is not adjacent to
d
. Then
b-a-c-d
is an induced
P_{4}
, a contradiction.
∎

The next lemma generalizes the
P_{4}
-structure condition.

Lemma 4.11
.

There is no induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
a\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
,
b,c\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
and
d\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
.

Proof.

If such a
P_{4}
exists, then
a-b-c-d-B_{i+1}-B_{i+2}-a
is an induced cyclic sequence. This contradicts Lemma
4.7
.
∎

For a subset
S
and two vertices
x,y\notin S
, we say that
x
and
y
are
comparable
or have
neighborhood containment in
S
if
N_{S}(x)\subseteq N_{S}(y)
or
N_{S}(y)\subseteq N_{S}(x)
, and have
disjoint neighborhoods in
S
if
N_{S}(x)\cap N_{S}(y)=\emptyset
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For
x,y\in A_{3}^{\prime}(i)
with
xy\notin E(G)
,
x
and
y
have neighborhood containment in one of
B_{i-1}
and
B_{i+1}
and have disjoint neighborhoods in the other.

Proof.

If
x
and
y
do not have neighborhood containment in either
B_{i-1}
or
B_{i+1}
, then there is an induced
C_{6}
. So we may assume that
x
and
y
have neighborhood containment in
B_{i-1}
. In particular,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor in
B_{i-1}
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
x
and
y
cannot have a common neighbor in
B_{i+1}
. So
x
and
y
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+1}
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Each pair of vertices in
A_{3}(i)
have comparable neighborhoods in
B_{i}
. This implies that
B_{i}
contains a vertex that is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i)

(
a non-neighbor of a largest vertex in
A_{3}(i)
)
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Assume that there are
u,v\in A_{3}(i)
and
s,s^{\prime}\in B_{i}
such that
us,vs^{\prime}\in E(G)
but
us^{\prime},vs\notin E(G)
.
Since
u-s-s^{\prime}-v-u
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
uv\notin E(G)
.
Then
u,v
have disjoint neighborhoods either in
B_{i+1}
or in
B_{i-1}
.
By symmetry, we may assume that
u,v
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-1}
.
Let
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in B_{i-1}
such that
uu^{\prime},vv^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
Since
u-s-s^{\prime}-v-v^{\prime}-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}
is not an induced
P_{7}
, either
v^{\prime}s\in E(G)
or
v^{\prime}s^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
If
v^{\prime}s^{\prime}\notin E(G)
, then
v^{\prime}s\notin E(G)
.
It follows that
v^{\prime}s^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
By symmetry,
u^{\prime}s\in E(G)
.
Since
s-s^{\prime}-v^{\prime}-u^{\prime}-s
is not an induced
C_{4}
, either
sv^{\prime}\in E(G)
or
s^{\prime}u^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
By symmetry, we may assume that
sv^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
Then
v^{\prime}
is a common neighbor of
v
and
s
.
Thus,
v
and
s
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+1}
.
Let
w
be a common neighbor of
u,v
in
B_{i+1}
.
Thus,
w
is not adjacent to
s
.
Then
w-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-v^{\prime}-s-u-w
is an induced
C_{6}
.
∎

In the following, we often say that
a-B-c-\cdots-d
is an induced path where
B
is a subset of vertices. By that we mean any vertex in
B
can be taken on the path so that the resulting sequence is an induced path. The same notation applies for induced cycles.

Lemma 4.14
.

The following properties hold for
A_{5}
.

(1)

A_{5}
is complete to
V(H)
.

(2)

A_{5}
is a clique complete to
A_{3}
.

Proof.

Let
v\in A_{5}
.

(1) We first claim that
v
is complete to
q_{i}
. Suppose that
v
is not adjacent to
q_{i}
for some
i\in[5]
. As
q_{i}
is complete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
, it follows that
v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-q_{i}-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-v
form an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction. Now we show that
v
is complete to
V(H)
. Suppose that
v
is not adjacent to
w\in B_{i}
for some
i\in[5]
. Since
v
is adjacent to
q_{i-1}
and
q_{i+1}
,
v-q_{i-1}-w-q_{i+1}-v
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction.

(2) Suppose that
v
is not adjacent to
w\in A_{3}(i)\cup A_{5}
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i-1}}(w)
and
b\in N_{B_{i+1}}(w)
. By (1), we obtain
va,vb\in E(G)
and so
v-a-w-b-v
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

4.2
Anticomplete pairs

In this subsection, we prove that certain sets are anticomplete to each other.

Lemma 4.15
.

The following holds.

(1)

Let
u\in A_{1}
and
v\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
such that
\operatorname{supp}(u)\cap\operatorname{supp}(v)=\emptyset
and
\{u,v\}
is anticomplete to at least two consecutive sets
B_{j}
and
B_{j+1}
. Then
uv\notin E(G)
.

(2)

A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(j)
for
i\neq j
, and
A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
.

Proof.

The second statement follows immediately from the first statement. So it remains to prove (1). We may assume that
u\in A_{1}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
and
\{u,v\}
is anticomplete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i}}(u)
. Suppose to the contrary that
uv\in E(G)
.
If
v
has a neighbor
b
in
B_{i+2}
, then
v-u-a-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-b-v
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction. So
v
is anticomplete to
B_{i+2}
. It follows that
v
has a neighbor in
B_{i+1}
and let
b\in N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
.
If
ab\in E(G)
, then
u-v-b-a-u
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
ab\notin E(G)
, then
v-u-a-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-b-v
is an induced
C_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in A_{1}(i)
and
v\in A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
. Suppose that
uv\in E(G)
. By symmetry, we may assume
v\in A_{3}(i+2)
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i}}(u)
. Let
b\in N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
and
c\in N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
. If
a
has a neighbor
x
in
N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
, then
a-x-v-u-a
is an induced
C_{4}
. So,
a
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
and this implies that
a
has a neighbor
y
in
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. Then
v-b-y-a-q_{i-1}-c-v
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.17
.

A_{2}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i+1)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in A_{2}(i)
and
v\in A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i+1)
. Suppose that
uv\in E(G)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
v\in A_{2}(i+1)
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i}}(u)
and
b\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Then
u-v-b-q_{i-2}-q_{i-1}-a-u
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
u,v\in V(G)\setminus V(H)
such that

•

u
has a neighbor in each of
B_{i-1}
and
B_{i}
, and is anticomplete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}
,

•

v
has a neighbor in each of
B_{i-2}
and
B_{i+2}
, and is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}
.

Then
uv\notin E(G)
.
This implies the following.

•

A_{3}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
.

•

A_{3}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+2)
.

•

A_{2}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{2}(i+2)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
uv\in E(G)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
and
b\in N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
. Then
a
has a neighbor
c
in
B_{i-2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
and
b
has a neighbor
d
in
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(u)
. By Lemma
4.1
(1),
cd\in E(G)
. If
c
has a neighbor
c^{\prime}
in
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
d
has a neighbor
d^{\prime}
in
N_{B_{i}}(u)
, then
u-v-c^{\prime}-c-d-d^{\prime}-u
is an induced
C_{6}
. So we may assume that either
c
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
or
d
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i}}(u)
. By symmetry, we assume that
c
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Then
c
has some neighbor
x
in
B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
and thus
u-a-c-x-N_{B_{i+2}}(v)-v-u
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.19
.

A_{3}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in A_{3}(i)
and
v\in A_{3}(i+1)\cup A_{3}(i-1)
. Suppose that
uv\in E(G)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
v\in A_{3}(i+1)
. By Lemma
2.2
,
N_{G}(u)
and
N_{G}(v)
are comparable within each of
B_{i}
and
B_{i+1}
. By Lemma
4.10
,
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
and
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
are not empty.

Case 1:
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
is not anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
.

Let
x\in B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
and
y\in N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
with
xy\in E(G)
. If
x
is adjacent to
v
, then
x-y-u-v-x
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
xv\notin E(G)
.

Assume that
x
has a neighbor
x^{\prime}\in B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. Then
vx^{\prime}\notin E(G)
and it follows that
x^{\prime}-x-y-u-v-B_{i+2}-x^{\prime}
contains an induced
C_{6}
or
C_{7}
depending on whether
v
and
x^{\prime}
have a common neighbor in
B_{i+2}
. So
x
is anticomplete to
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. By Lemma
4.10
,
x
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(u)
. Therefore,
x
has a neighbor
a\in N_{B_{i+1}}(v)\setminus N_{B_{i+1}}(u)
.

Let
z\in B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. It follows that
xz\notin E(G)
. Assume that
z
has a neighbor
z^{\prime}
in
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
. By Lemma
4.12
,
u
and
z^{\prime}
have some common neighbor
z^{\prime\prime}
in
B_{i-1}
. Then
v-u-z^{\prime\prime}-z^{\prime}-z-B_{i+2}-v
is an induced
C_{6}
or
C_{7}
depending on whether
v
and
z
have a common neighbor in
B_{i+2}
. Thus,
z
is anticomplete to
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
.

Assume that
z
has a neighbor
b\in N_{B_{i}}(u)\setminus N_{B_{i}}(v)
. If
ab\in E(G)
, then
u-b-a-v-u
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
ab\notin E(G)
, then
a-x-b-z-a
is an induced
C_{4}
. Therefore,
z
has a neighbor in
N_{B_{i}}(u)\cap N_{B_{i}}(v)
. By Lemma
4.12
,
z
and
v
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
. Let
c\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
d\in N_{B_{i+2}}(z)
. Note that
uz\notin E(G)
for otherwise
u-z-a-v-u
is an induced
C_{4}
. Therefore,
z-d-c-v-u-y-x
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.

Case 2:
B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Let
x\in B_{i}\setminus N_{G}(u)
and
z\in B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. Let
a\in N_{B_{i-1}}(u)
and
b\in N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
, and let
c\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
d\in N_{B_{i+2}}(z)
. If
uz\notin E(G)
, then
z-d-c-v-u-a-b
is an induced
P_{7}
. If
vx\notin E(G)
, then
x-b-a-u-v-c-d
is an induced
P_{7}
. Thus, we may assume that
uz,vx\in E(G)
. If
xz\notin E(G)
, then
b-x-v-u-z-d-q_{i-2}-b
is an induced
C_{7}
. Hence, we obtain
xz\in E(G)
, and then
u-z-x-v-u
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

A_{2}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i+2)\cup A_{3}(i-1)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in A_{2}(i)
and
v\in A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
. Suppose that
uv\in E(G)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
v\in A_{3}(i+2)
. By Lemma
4.10
,
B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
.

Case 1:

B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
is not anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
.

Let
x\in B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
have a neighbor
a\in N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
. By Lemma
4.10
(3),
x
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(v)
. Let
y\in B_{i+1}(x)
. If
uy\notin E(G)
, then
u-B_{i}-y-x-a-v-u
is an induced
C_{6}
or
C_{7}
depending on whether
u
and
y
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
. Thus,
uy\in E(G)
. By Lemma
4.10
(3), we obtain
q_{i+2}\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and it follows that
u-y-q_{i+2}-v-u
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction.

Case 2:

B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
.

Let
x\in B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(v)
. Recall that
q_{i+2}\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
. Then
u-v-q_{i+2}-N_{B_{i-2}}(x)-q_{i-1}-N_{B_{i}}(u)-u
is an induced
C_{6}
, a contradiction.
∎

5
A_{0}
is empty

In this section, we show that
A_{0}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
K
be a component of
G[A_{0}]
.

(1)

If
v\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, then
v
is complete or anticomplete to
K
.

(2)

If
v\in A_{3}
, then
v
is complete or anticomplete to
K
.

Proof.

We prove the properties one by one.

(1) Suppose that
v
has a neighbor in
B_{i}
and has no neighbors in
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}
. Let
c\in B_{i}
be a neighbor of
v
.
If
v
is adjacent to
a
but not adjacent to
b
with
ab\in E(K)
, then
b-a-v-c-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}
is an induced
P_{7}
.

(2) Let
v\in A_{3}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
. Suppose that
v
is adjacent to
a
but not adjacent to
b
with
ab\in E(K)
. By Lemma
4.10
(2),
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
or
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
. By symmetry, we assume that
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)
contains a vertex
c
. Let
d\in N(v)\cap B_{i+1}
. Then
a-b-v-d-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-c
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
K
be a component of
G[A_{0}]
.
Let
v\in A_{1}
and
w\in A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{5}
such that
v,w
have neighbors in
K
. Then
vw\in E(G)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
v\in A_{1}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
and
vw\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
5.1
,
v
is complete to
K
.
Let
z
be a common neighbor of
v
and
w
in
K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
v
and
w
have no
common neighbors in
H
. So
w\notin A_{5}
by Lemma
4.14
.
Let
a
be a neighbor of
v
in
B_{i}
. We divide cases depending on the place of
w
.

Case 1:

w\in A_{1}(i)\cup A_{1}(i\pm 1)\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
N_{G}(w)\cap V(H)
are contained in
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}
.
Note that
a
is not adjacent to
w
. Then

w-z-v-a-q_{i+1}-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}

is an induced
P_{7}
.

Case 2:

w\in A_{1}(i\pm 2)\cup A_{2}(i+2)\cup A_{3}(i\pm 2)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
w
has a neighbor
b
in
B_{i+2}
and
w
is anticomplete to
B_{i+1}
.
Then
v-z-w-b-q_{i+1}-a-v
is an induced
C_{6}
.

Case 3:

w\in A_{2}(i+1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i\pm 1)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that

w
has a neighbor
b
in
B_{i+2}
and
w
is anticomplete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}
. Then

a-v-z-w-b-q_{i-2}-q_{i-1}-a

is an induced
C_{7}
.

Case 4:

w\in A_{3}(i)
.

By Lemma
4.10
, either
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
or
B_{i+1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
.
By symmetry, we assume
B_{i-1}\setminus N_{G}(v)\neq\emptyset
. Let
b
be a neighbor of
w
in
B_{i+1}
and
c
be a non-neighbor of
w
in
B_{i-1}
. Then

v-z-w-b-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-c

is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
K
be a component of
G[A_{0}]
.
Let
v\in A_{2}
and
w\in A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{5}
such that
v,w
have neighbors in
K
. Then
vw\in E(G)
.

Proof.

Suppose
v\in A_{2}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
and
vw\notin E(G)
.
Let
z
be a common neighbor of
v
and
w
in
K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
v
and
w
have no
common neighbor in
H
. So
w\notin A_{5}
by Lemma
4.14
.
We divide cases depending on the place of
w
.

Case 1:

w\in A_{2}(i)
.

Let
a
be a neighbor of
v
in
B_{i+1}
.
Then

w-z-v-a-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-q_{i-1}

is an induced
P_{7}
.

Case 2:

w\in A_{2}(i\pm 1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
w
has a neighbor in
B_{i-1}
and it is anticomplete to
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i-2}
.
Let
a
be a neighbor of
v
in
B_{i+1}
and
b
be a neighbor of
w
in
B_{i-1}
. Then

v-z-w-b-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-a-v
is an induced
C_{7}
.

Case 3:

w\in A_{2}(i\pm 2)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
.

By symmetry, we may assume that
w
has a neighbor in
B_{i-2}
and
w
is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}
.
Let
a
be a neighbor of
v
in
B_{i}
and
b
be a neighbor of
w
in
B_{i-2}
. Then
v-z-w-b-q_{i-1}-a-v
is an induced
C_{6}
.

Case 4:

w\in A_{3}(i-2)
.

By Lemma
4.10
,
B_{i-2}\setminus N_{G}(w)
contains a vertex
a
. By Lemma
4.10
and symmetry, we may assume that
a
is anticomplete to
N_{G}(w)\cap B_{i+2}
. This implies that
a
has a neighbor
c
in
B_{i+2}\setminus N_{G}(w)
.
Then
v-z-w-b-a-c-q_{i+1}
is a bad
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
K
be a component of
G[A_{0}]
.
Let
v,w\in A_{3}
such that
v,w
have neighbors in
K
. Then
vw\in E(G)
.

Proof.

Suppose
v\in A_{3}(i)
for some
i\in[5]
and
vw\notin E(G)
.
Let
z
be a common neighbor of
v
and
w
in
K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
v
and
w
have no
common neighbors in
H
. We divide cases depending on the place of
w
.

Case 1:

w\in A_{3}(i)
.

This is impossible by Lemma
4.12
.

Case 2:

w\in A_{3}(i\pm 1)
.

By symmetry, we assume that
w\in A_{3}(i+1)
.
Let
a\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
v
and
b\in B_{i+2}
be a neighbor of
w
. Then
z-v-a-q_{i-2}-b-w-z
is an induced
C_{6}
.

Case 3:

w\in A_{3}(i\pm 2)
.

By symmetry, we assume that
w\in A_{3}(i+2)
.
Let
a\in B_{i+1}
be a neighbor of
v
and
b\in B_{i+1}
be a neighbor of
w
.
Let
c\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
v
. If
c
and
w
have a common neighbor
d\in B_{i-2}
, then
v-a-b-w-d-c-v
is an induced
C_{6}
. Otherwise,
v-a-b-w-d-e-c-v
is an induced
C_{7}
where
d\in B_{i-2}
is a neighbor of
w
and
e\in B_{i-2}
is a neighbor of
c
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

A_{0}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

If not, then
G
has a clique cutset
by Lemmas
5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
and
4.14
.
∎

6
Neighborhoods of
A_{1}(i)

Our main result in this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
G[A_{1}(i)]
.
Then
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
is a clique.

We start with some auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
G[A_{1}(i)]
. Each vertex in
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
is either complete or anticomplete to
K
.

Proof.

Let
v\in A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
be a vertex that has a neighbor in
K
. By symmetry, we may assume that
v\in N(K)\cap A_{2}(i)
. We show that
v
is complete to
K
. Suppose not. Then there exists an edge
ab\in E(K)
such that
vb\in E(G)
and
va\notin E(G)
. It follows that
a-b-v-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-q_{i-1}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
G[A_{1}(i)]
. Each vertex in
A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
is either complete or anticomplete to
K
.

Proof.

Let
v\in A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
be a vertex that has a neighbor in
K
. Suppose that there exists an edge
ab\in E(K)
such that
vb\in E(G)
and
va\notin E(G)
. We first assume that
v\in A_{3}(i)
. If
B_{i+1}\setminus N(v)\neq\emptyset
, then
a-b-v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-x
is an induced
P_{7}
. So,
v
is complete to
B_{i+1}
. By symmetry,
v
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. This contradicts Lemma
4.10
(2).

We now assume that
v\in A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
v\in A_{3}(i-1)
. By Lemma
4.10
(3),
B_{i-1}\setminus N(v)
is anticomplete to either
N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
or
N_{B_{i}}(v)
. If
B_{i-1}\setminus N(v)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(v)
, then
a-b-v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So,
B_{i-1}\setminus N(v)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i}}(v)
. It follows that
a-b-v-N_{B_{i-2}}(v)-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is a bad
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
G[A_{1}(i)]
. The following are satisfied.

(1)

If
A_{2}(i-1)\neq\emptyset
and
A_{2}(i)\neq\emptyset
, then
K
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
.

(2)

K
is anticomplete to either
A_{2}(i)
or
A_{3}(i-1)
. Similarly,
K
is anticomplete to either
A_{2}(i-1)
or
A_{3}(i+1)
.

(3)

K
is anticomplete to either
A_{3}(i-1)
or
A_{3}(i+1)
.

(4)

K
is anticomplete to either
A_{3}(i-1)
or
A_{3}(i)
. Similarly,
K
is anticomplete to either
A_{3}(i)
or
A_{3}(i+1)
.

(5)

K
has neighbors in at most one of
A_{3}(i-1),A_{3}(i),A_{3}(i+1)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
u\in A_{2}(i-1)
and
v\in A_{2}(i)
be vertices that have a neighbor in
K
. By Lemma
4.17
,
uv\notin E(G)
. If
u
and
v
have a common neighbor
w
in
K
, then
w-v-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-N_{B_{i-1}}(u)-u-w
is an induced
C_{7}
. So,
u
and
v
have disjoint neighborhood in
K
. Then,
(N(u)\cap K)-u-N_{B_{i-1}}(u)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-v
and
(N(v)\cap K)-v-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-N_{B_{i-1}}(u)-u
are an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.

(2) Let
u\in A_{3}(i-1)
and
v\in A_{2}(i)
be vertices that have a neighbor in
K
. By Lemma
4.20
,
uv\notin E(G)
. Let
P
be a shortest path from
N(u)\cap V(K)
to
N(v)\cap V(K)
in
K
. Then
u-P-v-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-q_{i+2}-N_{B_{i-2}}(u)-u
is an induced cycle of length at least
6
, a contradiction.

(3) Let
u\in A_{3}(i-1)
and
v\in A_{3}(i+1)
be vertices that have a neighbor in
K
. By Lemma
4.18
,
uv\notin E(G)
. Let
P
be a shortest path from
N(u)\cap V(K)
to
N(v)\cap V(K)
in
K
. By Lemma
4.10
, we obtain
B_{i-1}\setminus N(u)\neq\emptyset
and
B_{i+1}\setminus N(v)\neq\emptyset
. Thus, it follows that
(B_{i-1}\setminus N(u))-N_{B_{i-1}}(u)-u-P-v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-(B_{i+1}\setminus N(v))
is an induced path of order at least
7
, a contradiction.

(4) Let
u\in A_{3}(i-1)
and
v\in A_{3}(i)
be vertices that have a neighbor in
K
. By Lemma
4.19
,
uv\notin E(G)
. Let
P
be a shortest path from
N(u)\cap V(K)
to
N(v)\cap V(K)
in
K
. Then
u-P-v-N_{B_{i+1}}(v)-q_{i+2}-N_{B_{i-2}}(u)-u
is an induced cycle of length at least
6
, a contradiction.

(5) This follows from (3) and (4).
∎

We are now ready to prove Proposition
6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We may assume by Lemma
6.4
that
K
has no neighbor in
A_{2}(i)
and has neighbors in at most one of
A_{3}(i-1),A_{3}(i),A_{3}(i+1)
.
So
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))\subseteq B_{i}\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(j)\cup A_{5}
for some
j\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}
.

(1) Each vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{5}
is universal in
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
.

Let
x\in N(K)\cap A_{5}
and
y\in N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-1)
be non-adjacent. By Lemma
6.2
,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor
z
in
K
. By Lemma
4.14
,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor
w\in B_{i-1}
and it follows that
z-y-w-x-z
is an induced
C_{4}
, a contradiction. So
N(K)\cap A_{5}
is complete to
N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-1)
.
The statement follows from Lemma
4.14
. This proves (1).

(2) Each vertex in
N(K)\cap B_{i}
is universal in
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
.

Let
x\in N_{B_{i}}(K)
and
y\in N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
be non-adjacent.
By symmetry, we may assume that
y\in A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)
.
By Lemma
6.2
,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor
z
in
K
.
Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
N_{B_{j}}(y)
is anticomplete to
x
for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
.
Then,
N_{B_{i-1}}(y)-y-z-x-q_{i+1}-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}
is a bad
P_{7}
. This proves (2).

(3) Each vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-1)
is universal in
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
.

Suppose that
x\in N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-1)
and
y\in N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
are non-adjacent. By Lemma
6.2
,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor
z
in
K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
N_{B_{j}}(x)
and
N_{B_{j}}(y)
are disjoint for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
. If
y\in A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
, then
y-z-x-N_{B_{i-1}}(x)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
y\in A_{3}(i-1)
.
If
B_{i}\setminus N(y)\neq\emptyset
, then
x-z-y-N_{B_{i-2}}(y)-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}-B_{i}\setminus N(y)
is a bad
P_{7}
. So,
y
is complete to
B_{i}
and this implies that
B_{i-1}\setminus N(y)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(y)
by Lemma
4.10
(3). Then
x-z-y-N_{B_{i-1}}(y)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
This proves (3).

(4) Each vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{3}(j)
is universal in
N(K)\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2))
for each
j\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}
.

Suppose that
x,y\in N(K)\cap A_{3}(j)
are non-adjacent. By Lemma
6.3
,
x
and
y
have a common neighbor in
K
and so
x
and
y
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{j}
for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
by
C_{4}
-freeness of
G
.
By Lemma
4.13
,
j=i
.
This contradicts Lemma
4.12
.
This proves (4).

The proposition follows from (1)-(4).
∎

We conclude this section with two more lemmas.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
a-b-c
be an induced
P_{3}
in
A_{1}(i)
. Then
N_{B_{i}}(a)\subseteq N_{B_{i}}(b)
and
N_{B_{i}}(c)\subseteq N_{B_{i}}(b)
.

Proof.

Suppose the lemma does not hold. Then there exists a vertex
p\in B_{i}
that is adjacent to
a
but not to
b
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
pc\notin E(G)
. Then
c-b-a-p-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
K
be a component of
A_{1}(i-1)
. Then
N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-2)
is complete to
B_{i-2}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1
,
K
has a neighbor
v^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i+1)
. Let
v\in K
be a neighbor of
v^{\prime}
. Let
w\in N(K)\cap A_{2}(i-2)
. By Lemma
6.2
, we have
vw\in E(G)
. If
w
has non-neighbor
x
in
B_{i-2}
, then
x-N_{B_{i-2}}(w)-w-v-v^{\prime}-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

7
Neighborhoods of
A_{2}(i)

In this section, we present three more lemmas on
A_{2}(i)
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
A_{2}(i)
. Then
K
is anticomplete to either
A_{3}(i)
or
A_{3}(i+1)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in A_{3}(i)
and
v\in A_{3}(i+1)
be vertices that have a neighbor in
K
. By Lemma
4.19
, we have
uv\notin E
. This contradicts Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
A_{2}(i)
. If two vertices
u,v\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)
are non-adjacent, then
u
and
v
have a common neighbor in
K
.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
u
and
v
do not have a common neighbor in
K
. Let
P
be a shortest path from
u
and
v
with internal in
K
. Note that
P
has at least
4
vertices.
By Lemma
4.8
,
u
and
v
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
and so have disjoint neighbors in
B_{i+2}
.
Then
u-P-v-N_{B_{i+2}}(v)-q_{i-2}-q_{i-1}
is an induced path of with at least
7
vertices, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
a-b-c
be an induced
P_{3}
in
A_{2}(i)
. Then
N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(j)}(a)\subseteq N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(j)}(b)
and
N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(j)}(c)\subseteq N_{A^{\prime}_{3}(j)}(b)
for
j\in\{i,i+1\}
.

Proof.

By symmetry, we may assume that
j=i
. Suppose the lemma does not hold. Then there exists a vertex
p\in B_{i}
that is adjacent to
a
but not to
b
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
pc\notin E(G)
. Then
c-b-a-p-N_{B_{i-1}}(p)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

8
Neighborhoods of
A_{3}(i)

In this section, we prove more properties on
A_{3}(i)
.

8.1
Single
A_{3}(i)

In this subsection, we prove properties for a single
A_{3}(i)
. We start with a single vertex in
A_{3}(i)
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
x\in A_{3}(i)
and suppose that
B_{i}\setminus N(x)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(x)
. Then the following properties hold.

(1)

q_{i}\in N_{B_{i}}(x)
and
q_{i+1}\in B_{i+1}\setminus N(x)
.

(2)

N_{B_{i}}(x)
is complete to
N_{B_{j}}(x)
for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
.

(3)

For any vertex
a\in B_{i}\setminus N(x)
and
b\in N_{B_{i}}(x)
, then
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(a)\subset N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}}(b)
.

(4)

For any vertex
a\in B_{i-1}\setminus N(x)
and
b\in N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
, then
N_{B_{i-2}\cup B_{i}}(a)\subseteq N_{B_{i-2}\cup B_{i}}(b)
. This implies that there is a vertex in
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
complete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i}
.

Proof.

We prove these one by one.

(1) This follows from the definition of
q_{i}
and
q_{i+1}
.

(2) By (1),
q_{i+1}x\notin E(G)
.
If
a\in N_{B_{i}}(x)
and
b\in N_{B_{i+1}}(x)
are non-adjacent, then
x-a-q_{i+1}-b-x
is an induced
C_{4}
.
If
a\in N_{B_{i}}(x)
and
b\in N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
are non-adjacent, then
b-x-a-q_{i+1}
contradicts Lemma
4.11
.

(3) By definition,
x
has a neighbor
c\in B_{i+1}
. By (2),
c
is adjacent to
b
but not to
a
. So we are done by Lemma
4.1
(1).

(4) By Lemma
4.11
,
N_{B_{i-2}}(a)\subseteq N_{B_{i-2}}(b)
. If there is a vertex
c\in B_{i}
adjacent to
a
but not to
b
, then
c\in B_{i}\setminus N(x)
by (2), and so there exist vertices
d,e\in B_{i+1}
such that
x-b-a-c-d-e-x
is an induced
C_{6}
by the assumption on
x
.
∎

We then consider edges in
A_{3}(i)
. Let
B_{i\pm 1}=B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
xy
be an edge in
A_{3}(i)
. Then

(1)

N_{B_{i\pm 1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i\pm 1}}(y)
or
N_{B_{i\pm 1}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i\pm 1}}(x)
.

(2)

For
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
,
N_{B_{i}\cup B_{j}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i}\cup B_{j}}(y)
or
N_{B_{i}\cup B_{j}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i}\cup B_{j}}(x)
.

(3)

N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)
or
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)
.

Proof.

We prove the properties one by one.

(1) This follows directly from Lemmas
2.2
and
4.11
.

(2) By symmetry, we prove for
j=i+1
. Suppose that the statement is not true. Let
z\in B_{i}
be a neighbor of
x
but not a neighbor of
y
. Let
w
be a neighbor of
y
but not a neighbor of
x
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
w\in B_{i+1}
and
zw\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
4.13
, one can take a common non-neighbor
a\in B_{i}
of
x
and
y
. If
aw\notin E(G)
, then
a-z-x-y-w-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
aw\in E(G)
. Let
b\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
a
. By Lemma
4.10
,
by\notin E(G)
. By (1),
bx\notin E(G)
. Then

b-\{z,a\}-x-y-w-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-b
is an induced cyclic sequence.

(3) It follows directly from (1) and (2).
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
xy
be an edge in
A_{3}(i)
. Then
N_{B_{i}}(x)=N_{B_{i}}(y)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.2
(3), we may assume that
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)
.
Suppose that there exists a vertex
a\in B_{i}
with
ax\in E(G)
and
ay\notin E(G)
.
Let
d\in B_{i+1}
be a neighbor of
y
and
e\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
y
.
Since
N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(y)\subseteq N_{B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}}(x)
,
dx,ex\in E(G)
. By Lemma
8.1
(2),
ad,ae\in E(G)
. It follows that
a-d-y-e-a
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

We extend properties for edges to induced
P_{3}
s in
A_{3}(i)
.

Lemma 8.4
.

If
P=a-b-c
is an induced
P_{3}
in
A_{3}(i)
, then
N_{H}(a),N_{H}(c)\subseteq N_{H}(b)
.

Proof.

Since
ac\notin E(G)
,
a
and
c
have disjoint neighborhoods in one of
B_{i-1}
or
B_{i+1}
. So
N_{H}(a)
and
N_{H}(c)
cannot have a containment relation. This implies that
N_{H}(a),N_{H}(c)\subseteq N_{H}(b)
.
∎

We now prove the following key property.

Lemma 8.5

(
P_{4}
-freeness of
A_{3}(i)
)
.

A_{3}(i)
is
P_{4}
-free for each
i\in[5]
.

Proof.

Let
a-b-c-d
be an induced
P_{4}
in
A_{3}(i)
. By Lemma
8.4
to
a-b-c
and
b-c-d
, we have
N_{H}(a)\subseteq N_{H}(b)=N_{H}(c)
. This contradicts Lemma
4.12
.
∎

The next two lemmas are about components of
A_{3}(i)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For any component
K
of
A_{3}(i)
, there exists a vertex
x\in K
universal in
K
such that
N_{H}(K)=N_{H}(x)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.5
,
K
is
P_{4}
-free. It follows from
[
7
]
that
V(K)
can be partitioned into subsets
X
and
Y
such that
X
and
Y
are complete. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free, we may assume that
X
is a clique. Choose such a partition
(X,Y)
such that
|X|
is maximum.
By Lemma
8.2
, there exists
a vertex
x\in X
such that
N_{H}(x)
is maximum over all vertices in
X
.
For each vertex
y\in Y
,
y
has a non-neighbor
z\in Y
by the choice of
(X,Y)
. Applying Lemma
8.4
to
y-x-z
, we have
N_{H}(y),N_{H}(z)\subseteq N_{H}(x)
.
So
x
is a desired vertex.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
K
be a component of
A_{3}(i)
such that
B_{i}\setminus N(K)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(K)
. The set
X_{i-1}
of vertices in
B_{i-1}\setminus N(K)
having a neighbor in
B_{i}\setminus N(K)
is complete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.6
, there exists a vertex
u\in K
such that
N_{H}(K)=N_{H}(u)
.
Let
x\in X_{i-1}
and
a\in B_{i}\setminus N(K)
be a neighbor of
x
.
If
x
has a non-neighbor
x^{\prime}
in
B_{i-2}
, then
x^{\prime}-q_{i-2}-x-a-N_{B_{i+1}}(a)-N_{B_{i+1}}(u)-u
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
X_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i-2}
. If
x
has a non-neighbor
x^{\prime\prime}
in
N_{B_{i}}(u)
, then
x-a-x^{\prime\prime}-b
is a
P_{4}
-structure where
b\in N_{B_{i+1}}(u)
. So
X_{i-1}
is complete to
N_{B_{i}}(u)
. If
x
has a non-neighbor
x^{\prime\prime\prime}\in B_{i}\setminus N(K)
, then
x^{\prime\prime\prime}-a-x-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-N_{B_{i+1}}(u)-u
is an induced
P_{7}
. Therefore,
X_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i}
.
∎

The next lemma is used for coloring in Section
13
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Suppose that
H
is maximal.
For any component
K
of
A_{3}(i)
,
|B_{i}\setminus N(K)|\geq\omega(K)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.3
, all vertices in
K
have the same neighborhood in
B_{i}
. Suppose that
|B_{i}\setminus N(K)|<\omega(K)
. Let
M
be a maximum clique of
K
. Then
B^{\prime}_{i}=N_{B_{i}}(K)\cup M
is a clique of size bigger than
B_{i}
. Moreover,
|M|=\omega(K)\geq 2
. Let
x
and
y
be two vertices in
M
.
We now show that
(B_{1},\cdots,B^{\prime}_{i},\cdots,B_{5})
is still a nice blowup of
C_{5}
, which contradicts the maximality of
H
. Since each vertex in
B_{i}\setminus N(K)
is not complete to
N_{B_{i+1}}(K)
or
N_{B_{i-1}}(K)
by Lemma
4.10
,
q_{i}\in N_{B_{i}}(K)
. Therefore, it suffices to check the
P_{4}
-structure condition.

Case 1
:
B_{i-1}
(and
B_{i+1}
).

If there exists an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-x
with
a\in B_{i-2}
and
b,c\in B_{i-1}
, then
a-b-c-x-N_{B_{i+1}}(x)-q_{i+2}-a
is an induced
C_{6}
. So the
P_{4}
-structure condition is satisfied for
B_{i-1}
. By symmetry, the
P_{4}
-structure condition is satisfied for
B_{i+1}
.

Case 2
:
B_{i}^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
a-b-c-d
is an induced
P_{4}
with
a\in B_{i-1}
,
b,c\in B^{\prime}_{i}
, and
d\in B_{i+1}
. By Lemma
8.2
and niceness of
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{5})
, exactly one of
x
and
y
is on this
P_{4}
. We may assume that
b=x
. If
c\in N_{B_{i}}(K)
, then
ac\in E(G)
by Lemma
8.1
(2), which contradicts the assumption. Thus, we may assume that
c=y
, which contradicts Lemma
8.2
.
Therefore, the
P_{4}
-structure condition is satisfied for
B^{\prime}_{i}
.
∎

8.2
Multiple
A_{3}(i)

In this subsection, we prove properties involving multiple
A_{3}(j)
s. Recall that
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)=A_{3}(i)\cup B_{i}
for each
i\in[5]
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
v,a\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
be non-adjacent, and
b,w\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)
be non-adjacent. Then it cannot be the case that
N_{B_{i}}(v)
and
N_{B_{i}}(a)
are disjoint, and that
N_{B_{i}}(w)
and
N_{B_{i}}(b)
are disjoint.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.11
, every
x\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
and
y\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)
have neighborhood containment in
B_{i}
.
If
N_{B_{i}}(v)
and
N_{B_{i}}(a)
are disjoint, then each of
N_{B_{i}}(w)
and
N_{B_{i}}(b)
contains both
N_{B_{i}}(v)
and
N_{B_{i}}(a)
. This implies that
w
and
b
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
x,y\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
be two non-adjacent vertices such that
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
and
N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
are disjoint. Then
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
is complete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
.

Proof.

We first show that each vertex
v\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
is either complete or anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
. Suppose that
v
has a neighbor
a\in N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
. If
v
has a non-neighbor
b\in N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
, then
v-a-b-y
contradicts Lemma
4.11
. So
v
is complete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
. It follows that
v
is complete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
. Now suppose that
v
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
. Let
c\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
v
and
d\in N_{B_{i-1}}(x)
. Then
v-c-d-x
contradicts Lemma
4.11
. So
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
is complete to
N_{B_{i-1}}(x)\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(y)
.
∎

Lemma 8.11
.

The neighbors of
A_{3}(i+2)
in
B_{i-2}
are complete to
A_{3}(i-2)
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Let
w\in B_{i-2}
be a neighbor of
v^{\prime}\in A_{3}(i+2)
and have a non-neighbor

v\in A_{3}(i-2)
.
Assume first that
v
and
w
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
.
By Lemma
4.12
,

v
and
w
have a common neighbor
c
in
B_{i-1}
.
Let
u\in B_{i+2}
be a neighbor of
v
.
By Lemma
8.1
(2),
uv^{\prime}\notin E(G)
.
Then
u-v-c-w-v^{\prime}-B_{i+1}-u
is an induced induced cyclic sequence.
So we may assume that
v
and
w
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-1}
.
If
v^{\prime}
is not complete to
B_{i+1}
, then
v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-N_{B_{i-1}}(w)-w-v^{\prime}-N_{B_{i+1}}(v^{\prime})-(B_{i+1}\setminus N(v^{\prime}))
is an induced
P_{7}
.
So
v^{\prime}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
.
Then
v-N_{B_{i-1}}(v)-N_{B_{i-1}}(w)-w-v^{\prime}-q_{i+1}-(B_{i+2}\setminus N(v^{\prime}))
is a bad
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
v\in A_{3}(i-2)
and
u\in A_{3}(i+2)
such that
B_{i-2}\setminus N(v)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
B_{i+2}\setminus N(u)
is anticomplete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(u)
.
Then

(1)

B_{i-2}\setminus N(v)
is anticomplete to
B_{i+2}\setminus N(u)
.

(2)

For any
w\in B_{i-2}\setminus N(v)
and
w^{\prime}\in B_{i+2}\setminus N(u)
,

N_{B_{i+2}}(w)
is complete to
N_{B_{i-2}}(w^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We prove one by one.

(1)
Let
v^{\prime}\in N_{B_{i+2}}(v)
and
u^{\prime}\in N_{B_{i-2}}(u)
.
Then
v^{\prime}w,u^{\prime}w^{\prime}\notin E(G)
.
By Lemma
8.11
,
v^{\prime}u,u^{\prime}v\in E(G)
.
By Lemma
8.1
(2),
v^{\prime}u^{\prime}\in E(G)
.
If
w\in B_{i-2}\setminus N(v)
and
w^{\prime}\in B_{i+2}\setminus N(u)
with
ww^{\prime}\in E(G)
, then
w-w^{\prime}-v^{\prime}-u^{\prime}-w
is an induced
C_{4}
.
This proves (1).

(2)
Suppose that there are
s\in N_{B_{i+2}}(w)
and
s^{\prime}\in N_{B_{i-2}}(w^{\prime})
with
ss^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. Then
w-s-w^{\prime}-s^{\prime}-w
is an induced
C_{4}
.
This proves (2).
∎

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
a_{1},b_{1}
be two non-adjacent vertices in
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
and
a_{2},b_{2}
be two non-adjacent vertices in
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
such that
N_{B_{i-2}}(a_{1})
and
N_{B_{i-2}}(b_{1})
are disjoint, and
N_{B_{i-1}}(a_{2})
and
N_{B_{i-1}}(b_{2})
are disjoint.
Then
N_{B_{i-2}}(a_{1})\cup N_{B_{i-2}}(b_{1})\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(a_{2})\cup N_{B_{i-1}}(b_{2})
is a clique.

Proof.

Let
K_{a_{1}}=N_{B_{i-2}}(a_{1})
,
K_{a_{2}}=N_{B_{i-1}}(a_{2})
,
K_{b_{1}}=N_{B_{i-2}}(b_{1})
and
K_{b_{2}}=N_{B_{i-1}}(b_{2})
.
Note that
K_{a_{j}}\cup K_{b_{j}}
is a clique for
j=1,2
.
By symmetry, we may assume that
b_{1}\in A_{3}(i-1)
and
b_{2}\in A_{3}(i-2)
.
We show that
K_{a_{1}}\cup K_{b_{1}}
is complete to
K_{a_{2}}\cup K_{b_{2}}
.
By Lemma
8.11
,
K_{b_{1}}\subseteq N(b_{2})
.
By Lemma
8.1
(2),
K_{b_{1}}
is complete to
K_{b_{2}}
.
Suppose first that
K_{a_{1}}
is not complete to
K_{b_{2}}
.
Then
a_{1}\in B_{i-1}
.
Let
s\in K_{a_{1}}
and
s^{\prime}\in K_{b_{2}}
with
ss^{\prime}\notin E(G)
.
Since
s^{\prime}a_{1}\in E(G)
,
a_{1}-s-K_{b_{1}}-s^{\prime}-a_{1}
is an induced
C_{4}
.
So
K_{a_{1}}
is complete to
K_{b_{2}}
.
By symmetry,
K_{a_{2}}
is complete to
K_{b_{1}}
.
Suppose that
K_{a_{1}}
is not complete to
K_{a_{2}}
.
Then we may assume that
a_{1}\in B_{i-1}
and
a_{2}\in B_{i-2}
.
By Lemma
8.12
(2),
K_{a_{1}}
is complete to
K_{a_{2}}
, a contradiction.
∎

8.3
Small vertex argument for
A_{3}(i)

In this subsection, we apply the small vertex argument for some vertices in
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
to conclude that certain sets have large sizes.

Lemma 8.14

(Small vertex argument for
A_{3}(i)
)
.

Let
v
be a
(A_{3}(i),H)
-minimal simplicial vertex.
If
v
has no neighbors in
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, then both
N(v)\setminus B_{i-1}
and
N(v)\setminus B_{i+1}
are cliques and so
|N_{B_{i-1}}(v)|,|N_{B_{i+1}}(v)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
By symmetry, we may assume that there are two non-adjacent vertices
s,s^{\prime}
in
N(v)\setminus B_{i-1}
.
Note that
N(v)\setminus B_{i-1}\subseteq B_{i+1}\cup B_{i}\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{5}
.
By Lemmas
4.14
,
8.1
(2) and
8.3
,
we may assume
s\in A_{3}(i)
and
s^{\prime}\in B_{i+1}
.
Suppose first that
s
is simplicial in
A_{3}(i)
. By the choice of
v
,
s
has a neighbor
s^{\prime\prime}\in B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
with
s^{\prime\prime}v\notin E(G)
. This contradicts Lemmas
2.2
and
4.11
.
So
s
is not simplicial in
A_{3}(i)
.
Then
s
has a neighbor
s^{\prime\prime}\in A_{3}(i)
with
s^{\prime\prime}v\notin E(G)
.
Now
s^{\prime\prime}-s-v
is an induced
P_{3}
in
A_{3}(i)
, which contradicts Lemma
8.4
.
∎

Lemma 8.15

(Small vertex argument for non-neighbors of
A_{3}(i)
in
B_{i}
)
.

Suppose that
A_{3}(i)\neq\emptyset
and
B_{i}
is anticomplete to
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
.
If
w\in B_{i}\setminus N(A_{3}(i))
has minimal neighborhood in
H
, then

N(w)\setminus B_{i-1}
and
N(w)\setminus B_{i+1}
are cliques, and so
|N_{B_{i-1}}(w)|,|N_{B_{i+1}}(w)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
N(w)\setminus B_{i+1}
is not a clique.
By Lemma
8.11
and
A_{3}(i)\neq\emptyset
,
N(w)\setminus B_{i+1}\subseteq B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup A_{5}
.
Let
s\in B_{i}
and
s^{\prime}\in B_{i-1}
be two non-adjacent neighbors of
w
.
By Lemma
8.1
(3),
s\in B_{i}\setminus N(A_{3}(i))
. By the minimality of
w
,
s
has a neighbor
s^{\prime\prime}\in B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
not adjacent to
w
.
It follows that
s^{\prime\prime}-s-w-s^{\prime}
is a
P_{4}
-structure if
s^{\prime\prime}\in B_{i+1}
or
s^{\prime\prime}-s-w-s^{\prime}-s^{\prime\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
if
s^{\prime\prime}\in B_{i-1}
.
∎

9
Non-neighbors of
C_{5}

For an induced
C_{5}=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}
and
X\subseteq V(C)
, we denote by
S(X)
the set of vertices
v\in V(G)\setminus V(C)
with
N_{C}(v)=X
.
We set
S_{0}=S(\emptyset)
and
S_{5}=S(V(C))
. For convenience, we omit the set notation
\{\}
when we write
X
in
S(X)
. For instance, we write
S(\{v_{1},v_{2}\})
as
S(v_{1},v_{2})
etc. The following lemma will be used to prove certain subgraphs are chordal in Section
11
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
C=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}
be an induced
C_{5}
of
G
and
K
be a non-clique component of
S_{0}
. Then the following holds for neighbors of
K
.

(1)

Every vertex in
N(K)\cap S(v_{i-2},v_{i+2})
is complete to
K
.

(2)

N(K)\cap S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
or
N(K)\cap S(v_{i+1},v_{i+2},v_{i+3})
is empty.

(3)

If
p,q\in N(K)
are not adjacent, then
p\in S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
and
q\in S(v_{i-2},v_{i+2})
for some
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{5}
.

(4)

N(K)
cannot contain 4 neighbors
p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}
such that
p\in S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
q\in S(v_{i-2},v_{i+2})
,
p^{\prime}\in S(v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i+2})
and
q^{\prime}\in S(v_{i-2},v_{i-1})
.

Proof.

We prove the properties one by one.

(1) Let
c\in N(K)\cap S(v_{i-2},v_{i+2})
be adjacent to
b\in K
. If
c
has a non-neighbor
a\in K
, then
a-b-c-v_{i-2}-v_{i-1}-v_{i}-v_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. This proves (1).

(2) Suppose for a contradiction that
a\in N(K)\cap S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
and
b\in N(K)\cap S(v_{i+1},v_{i+2},v_{i+3})
.
Since
a-v_{i-1}-v_{i-2}-b-a
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
ab\notin E(G)
.
Let
P
be a shortest path between
a
and
b
with internal in
K
.
Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
P
is an induced path on at least 4 vertices.
Then
a-v_{i-1}-v_{i-2}-b-P-a
is an induced cycle of length at least 6.
This proves (2).

(3) Since
G
has no clique cutsets,
N(K)
contains two non-adjacent vertices
p
and
q
.
Note first that if a vertex
a\in N(K)
has at most 2 neighbors on
C
, then
a
is complete to
K
for otherwise there is an induced
P_{7}
.
Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free and
K
is not a clique, we may assume that
p\in S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})\cup S_{5}
. Suppose first that
p\in S_{5}
. Since
S_{5}
is a clique complete to
S(v_{j-1},v_{j},v_{j+1})
for each
j\in[5]
, we have
q
has at two neighbors on
C
. It follows that
p
and
q
have a common neighbor in both
K
and
C
, a contradiction. So
p\in S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
.
Since
S_{5}
is complete to
p
, we have
q
has at most 3 neighbors on
C
.
By (2),
q\notin S(v_{i+1},v_{i+2},v_{i+3})
.
If
q\in S(v_{i-1},v_{i},v_{i+1})
,
p-v_{i-1}-q-v_{i+1}
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
q\in S(v_{i},v_{i+1},v_{i+2})
, then
p-v_{i-1}-v_{i-2}-v_{i+2}-q-K-p
is an induced cyclic sequence. So
q
has at most two neighbors on
C
. It follows that
p
and
q
have a common neighbor
u\in K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
p
and
q
have no common neighbors on
C
. If
q\in S(v_{i+2})
, then
p-v_{i-1}-v_{i-2}-v_{i+2}-q-N_{K}(p)-p
is an induced
C_{6}
. So
q\in S(v_{i-2},v_{i+2})
. This proves (3).

(4) Suppose not. It follows from (3) that
p-p^{\prime}-q-q^{\prime}-p
is an induced
C_{4}
