. This proves (4).
∎

10
A key technical lemma

In this section, we prove a key technical lemma.
Let
K
be a connected subgraph of
G
and
a,b\in N(K)
be non-adjacent. We say that a 6-tuple
(a,b,U,P_{a},P_{b},Q)
is
nice
if the following hold:

•

U,P_{a},P_{b},Q
form a partition of
K
where
U,P_{a},P_{b}\neq\emptyset
.

•

a
is complete to
P_{a}
and anticomplete to
P_{b}
, and
b
is complete to
P_{b}
and anticomplete to
P_{a}
.

•

P_{a},P_{b},Q
are pairwise anticomplete.

•

U
is complete to
P_{a}\cup P_{b}\cup\{a,b\}
.

Note that in a nice 6-tuple, the adjacency between
Q
and
\{a,b\}\cup U
can be arbitrary.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
G
be a
(P_{7},C_{4},C_{6},C_{7})
-free graph with no clique cutsets.
Let
K
be a non-empty connected subgraph of
G
with the following properties.

(1)

There is a subset
X\subseteq V(G)
such that each vertex in
N(K)\setminus X
is universal in
N(K)
.

(2)

K\cup X
contains no induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
d\in X
,
c\in K
and
a,b\in K\cup(N(K)\cap X)
.

(3)

There is a subset
Y\subseteq V(G)
anticomplete to
K
such that any two non-adjacent vertices in
N(K)\cap X
have non-empty, disjoint and complete neighborhoods in
Y
.

Then
N(K)\cap X
contains two non-adjacent vertices that are complete to
K
. In particular,
K
is a clique.

Proof.

By (1) and
G
has no clique cutsets,
N(K)\cap X
contains two non-adjacent vertices.

(i) If
N(a)\cap K\nsubseteq N(b)\cap K
and
N(b)\cap K\nsubseteq N(a)\cap K
where
a,b\in N(K)\cap X
are not adjacent, then there is a nice 6-tuple.

Let
U
be the set of common neighbors of
a
and
b
in
K
,
P_{a}
be the set of private neighbors of
a
in
K
,
P_{b}
be the set of private neighbors of
b
in
K
, and
Q
be the set of common non-neighbors of
a
and
b
in
K
. By assumption,
P_{a}
and
P_{b}
are not empty. We show that
(a,b,U,P_{a},P_{b},Q)
is nice. Let
a^{\prime}\in Y
be a neighbor of
a
and Let
b^{\prime}\in Y
be a neighbor of
b
. By (3),
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}\in E(G)
.

If
u\in U
is not adjacent to
p_{a}\in P_{a}
, then
p_{a}-a-u-b
is a forbidden
P_{4}
in (2). So
U
is complete to
P_{a}
. By symmetry,
U
is complete to
P_{b}
. If
p_{a}\in P_{a}
is adjacent to
p_{b}\in P_{b}
, then
p_{a}-a-a^{\prime}-b^{\prime}-b-p_{b}-p_{a}
is an induced
C_{6}
. So
P_{a}
and
P_{b}
are anticomplete. If
q\in Q
has a neighbor
p_{a}\in P_{a}
, then
q-p_{a}-a-a^{\prime}-b^{\prime}-b-p_{b}
is a bad
P_{7}
, since the only possible chord is
qp_{b}
. So
Q
is anticomplete to
P_{a}
and
P_{b}
. By connectivity of
K
, we have
U\neq\emptyset
. This completes the proof of (i).

(ii) For any non-adjacent vertices
a
and
b
in
N(K)
,

N(a)\cap K\subseteq N(b)\cap K
or
N(b)\cap K\subseteq N(a)\cap K
.

Suppose not. By (i), nice 6-tuples exist. We choose a nice 6-tuple
(a,b,U,P_{a},P_{b},Q)
so that
|P_{a}|+|P_{b}|
is minimum. We shall deduce a contradiction by applying the clique cutset argument to
P_{a}
. Let
p_{a}\in P_{a}
and
p_{b}\in P_{b}
.
Let
a^{\prime}\in Y
be a neighbor of
a
and
b^{\prime}\in Y
be a neighbor of
b
. By (3),
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}\in E(G)
.

Note that
N(P_{a})\subseteq(N(K)\setminus X)\cup(X\setminus\{b\})\cup U
by the definition of 6-nice tuple. Since all vertices in
N(K)\setminus X
are common neighbors of
a
and
b
and
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
U
is a clique complete to
N(K)\setminus X
.
If
u\in U
is not adjacent to
v\in N(P_{a})\cap X
, then
v-p_{a}-u-b
is a forbidden
P_{4}
in (2). So
U
is complete to
N(P_{a})\cap X
and thus each vertex in
U
is universal in
N(P_{a})
.

Next we show that

N(P_{a})\cap X
is complete to
a
.
Suppose by contradiction that
c\in N(P_{a})\cap X
is not adjacent to
a
. We may assume that
p_{a}c\in E(G)
. Note that
p_{b}-b-b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-a-p_{a}
is an induced
P_{6}
. Since
p_{b}-b-b^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-a-p_{a}-c
is not a bad
P_{7}
,
cb^{\prime}\in E(G)
. Since
c-U-b-b^{\prime}-c
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
cb\in E(G)
. Then
b-c-p_{a}-a
contradicts (2). This shows that
N(P_{a})\cap X
is complete to
a
.

Since
N(P_{a})
is not a clique, there exist vertices
c,c^{\prime}\in N(P_{a})\cap X
such that
c
and
c^{\prime}
are not adjacent. It follows that
ca,c^{\prime}a\in E(G)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
b
is not adjacent to
c
or
c^{\prime}
, say
c
.
Applying to
P_{b}
the statement
N(P_{a})\cap X
is complete to
a
, we have
c
is anticomplete to
P_{b}
.
If there is an edge
st
with
s\in N(c)\cap P_{a}
and
t\in P_{a}\setminus N(c)
, then
t-s-c-N_{Y}(c)-b^{\prime}-b-p_{b}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
N(c)\cap P_{a}
is anticomplete to
P_{a}\setminus N(c)
and thus
(c,b,U,P_{a}\cap N(c),P_{b},Q\cup(P_{a}\setminus N(c)))
is a nice 6-tuple. By the choice of
(a,b,U,P_{a},P_{b},Q)
,
c
is complete to
P_{a}
.
But now
c^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
P_{a}
and should be adjacent to
c
by the argument showing
N(P_{a})\cap X
is complete to
a
. This completes the proof of (ii).

(iii) Let
\{a,b\}
in
N(K)\cap X
such that
a
and
b
have maximum number of common neighbors in
K
. Then
K\subseteq N(a)\cap N(b)
.

By (ii), we may assume that
N(b)\cap K\subseteq N(a)\cap K
. Let
U
be the set of common neighbors in
K
,
P
be the private neighbors of
a
in
K
and
Q
be the set of common non-neighbors of
a
and
b
in
K
. Suppose that
P\cup Q
is not empty. Let
T
be a non-empty component of
P\cup Q
. Note that
N(T)\subseteq(N(K)\setminus X)\cup(X\setminus\{b\})\cup U
.
Since all vertices in
N(K)\setminus X
are common neighbors of
a
and
b
by (1),

U
is a clique complete to

N(K)\setminus X
.
Suppose that
u\in U
is not adjacent to
c\in N(T)\cap X
.
By (2),
c
and
u
have a common neighbor
d\in T
.
If
d\in P
, then
then
bc\in E(G)
by (ii) and so
c-d-u-b-c
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
d\in Q
, then
ca,cb\in E(G)
by (ii). It follows that
c-a-u-b-c
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
U
is complete to
N(T)\cap X
and so each vertex in
N(T)\cap U
is universal in
N(T)
.
Since
N(T)
is not a clique,
P\cup Q
has two non-adjacent neighbors
c,c^{\prime}
in
N(T)\cap X
. Since
b
is anticomplete to
T
, we have
b\neq c,c^{\prime}
. Since
T
is connected,
G
is
C_{6}
-free and
c,c^{\prime}
have disjoint and complete neighborhoods in
Y
, we have that
c,c^{\prime}
have a common neighbor in
T
. Then
\{c,c^{\prime}\}
contradicts the choice of
\{a,b\}
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

11
A_{1}
is not empty

In this section, we color
G
when
A_{1}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
C=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}
be an induced
C_{5}
of
G
such that
|S_{5}|
(see Section
9
) is minimum among all induced
C_{5}
s of
G
. Let
H
be a maximal nice blowup of
C
. From now on, we fix the choice of
C
and
H
. We first prove some general properties and then consider two cases depending the number of non-empty
A_{1}(i)
s (Subsections
11.1
and
11.2
).

Lemma 11.1
.

A_{5}
is complete to any edge
xy
with
x\in A_{1}(i)
and
y\in A_{2}(i+2)
for each
i\in[5]
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Let
xy
be an edge with
x\in A_{1}(i)
and
y\in A_{2}(i+2)
such that some
u\in A_{5}
is not complete to
\{x,y\}
.
Let
x^{\prime}\in N_{B_{i}}(x)
and
y^{\prime}\in N_{B_{i+2}}(y)
.
Then
C^{\prime}=x^{\prime}-q_{i+1}-y^{\prime}-y-x-x^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{5}
.
Let
S_{5}^{\prime}
be the set of vertices that are complete to
V(C^{\prime})
.
By definition of
S^{\prime}_{5}
,
S^{\prime}_{5}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{3}(i+1)\cup A_{5}
.
By Lemma
4.20
,
S^{\prime}_{5}\subseteq A_{5}
.
Since
A_{5}\subseteq S_{5}
and
u\notin S^{\prime}_{5}
,
|S^{\prime}_{5}|<|S_{5}|
which contradicts the choice of
C
.
∎

Lemma 11.2
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{5}
, either
A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+2)
or
A_{1}(i+1)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i-2)
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
x\in A_{1}(i)
be adjacent to
w\in A_{2}(i+2)
and
y\in A_{1}(i+1)
be adjacent to
z\in A_{2}(i-2)
. It follows from anticomplete pair properties that
xw
and
yz
induce a
2P_{2}
. By Lemma
4.9
,
w
and
z
have common neighbor
a
in
B_{i-2}
. Then
B_{i}-x-w-a-z-y-B_{i+1}-B_{i}
is an induced cyclic sequence.
∎

By Lemma
11.2
and Proposition
6.1
, there are at most 2 non-empty
A_{1}(i)
s. If there are two such
A_{1}(i)
s, their support are of distance 2 on the maximal nice blowup
H
.

Lemma 11.3
.

A_{1}(i)
is chordal for each
i\in[5]
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C^{\prime}=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}
is an induced
C_{5}
in
A_{1}(i)
. Let

N=B_{i}\cup A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)\cup A_{5}.

Note that
N(C^{\prime})\subseteq N
. Let
K
be the component of
G-(N(C^{\prime})\cup C)
containing
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
.
The notations
S(v_{3},v_{4})
and
S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
below are the same as defined in Section
9
.
Since
K
is not a clique,
K
has two non-adjacent neighbors
p
and
q
by Lemma
9.1
. By symmetry, we may assume that
p\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
and
q\in S(v_{3},v_{4})
. Since
q
is complete to
K
by Lemma
9.1
and each vertex in
N(C^{\prime})\setminus A_{5}
has a non-neighbor in
K
, we have
q\in A_{5}
.
If
p\in N(C^{\prime})\setminus(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2)\cup A_{5})
, then one can extend the induced
P_{4}=v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-p
to an induced
P_{7}
by using vertices in
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. So
p\in(A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i+2)\cup A_{5})
. Since
q\in A_{5}
is adjacent to any vertex in
A_{5}
or any vertex in
A_{2}(i+2)
that has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i)
and
pq\notin E(G)
, we have
p\in A_{1}(i)
.

Let
S
be the set of vertices in
K
that is a neighbor of any such vertex
p\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
. Since
A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
V(H)\setminus B_{i}
,
K\setminus S
contains
V(H)\setminus B_{i}
.
Let
L
be a component of
K\setminus S
containing
V(H)\setminus B_{i}
.
We show that
N(L)
is a clique. By Lemma
9.1
(3),
N(L)\setminus S
is a clique. Note that any vertex
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
L
for otherwise there is an induced
P_{7}
in
G
. Since any neighbor of a vertex in
A_{1}(i)
which is not in
A_{5}
has a non-neighbor in
L
,
N(L)\cap S\subseteq A_{5}
and so
N(L)\cap S
is a clique. Next we show that
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
N(L)\setminus S
. Suppose by contradiction that
a\in N(L)\cap S
and
b\in N(L)\setminus S
are not adjacent.
Since
a
is complete to
L
, we can take a common neighbor
c\in L
of
a
and
b
. By definition of
S
,
a
has a neighbor
r\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
. Since
r-a-c-b-r
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
br\notin E(G)
. If
b
is adjacent to both
v_{2}
and
v_{5}
, then
r-v_{2}-b-v_{5}-r
is an induced
C_{4}
. So we may assume that
bv_{2}\notin E(G)
by symmetry. Then
v_{4}-v_{3}-v_{2}-r-a-c-b
is a bad
P_{7}
. This proves that
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
N(L)\setminus S
and so
N(L)
is a clique, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.4
.

A_{2}(i-2)
is chordal for each
i\in[5]
.

Proof.

Suppose that
C^{\prime}=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}
is an induced
C_{5}
in
A_{2}(i-2)
. Let

N=B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{1}(i-2)\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{1}(i+1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{5}.

Note that
N(C^{\prime})\subseteq N
. Let
K
be the component of
G-(N(C^{\prime})\cup V(C^{\prime}))
containing
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. Since
K
is not a clique,
K
has two non-adjacent neighbors
p
and
q
by Lemma
9.1
.
The notations
S(v_{3},v_{4})
and
S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
below are the same as defined in Section
9
.
By symmetry, we may assume that
p\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
and
q\in S(v_{3},v_{4})
. Since
q
is complete to
K
by Lemma
9.1
and each vertex in
N(C^{\prime})\setminus A_{5}
has a non-neighbor in
K
, we have
q\in A_{5}
. By the choice of
C=C_{5}
and
q\in A_{5}
, we have

p\in A_{1}(i-2)\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
.

Let
S
be the set of vertices in
K
that is a neighbor of any such vertex
p\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
. Since
A_{1}(i-2)\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
is anticomplete to
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
, we have
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\subseteq K\setminus S
. Let
L
be the component of
K\setminus S
containing
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. We show that
N(L)
is a clique. By Lemma
9.1
,
N(L)\setminus S
is a clique. Note that any vertex
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
L
for otherwise there is an induced
P_{7}
in
G
. Since any vertex in
H\cup A_{1}\cup A_{2}
has a non-neighbor in
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
,
(N(L)\cap S)\cap(H\cup A_{1}\cup A_{2})=\emptyset
. Since any vertex in
A_{3}
has a non-neighbor in
B_{i}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
,
N(L)\cap S
contains no vertex from
A_{3}
. So
N(L)\cap S\subseteq A_{5}
and so is a clique.

Next we show that
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
N(L)\setminus S
. Suppose by contradiction that
a\in N(L)\cap S
and
b\in N(L)\setminus S
are not adjacent.
Since
a
is complete to
L
, we can take a common neighbor
c\in L
of
a
and
b
. By definition of
S
,
a
has a neighbor
r\in S(v_{5},v_{1},v_{2})
. Since
r-a-c-b-r
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
br\notin E(G)
. If
b
is adjacent to both
v_{2}
and
v_{5}
, then
r-v_{2}-b-v_{5}-r
is an induced
C_{4}
. So we may assume that
bv_{2}\notin E(G)
by symmetry. Then
v_{4}-v_{3}-v_{2}-r-a-c-b
is a bad
P_{7}
. This proves that
N(L)\cap S
is complete to
N(L)\setminus S
and so
N(L)
is a clique, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.5
.

For any two non-adjacent vertices
u,v\in A_{3}(i)\cup B_{i}
,
u
and
v
cannot have a common neighbor in
A_{1}(i)
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
x\in A_{1}(i)
be a common neighbor of
u
and
v
. Since
A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1}
and
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
u
and
v
have disjoint neighborhoods in each of
B_{i-1}
and
B_{i+1}
. This contradicts Lemma
4.12
.
∎

Lemma 11.6
.

Any component of
A_{1}(i)\setminus N(A_{2}(i+2))
is complete to a pair of non-adjacent vertices in
N(A_{2}(i+2))\cap A_{1}(i)
and so is a clique.

Proof.

Let
S=N(A_{2}(i+2))\cap A_{1}(i)
.
Let
K
be a component of
A_{1}(i)\setminus S
. Let
N=B_{i}\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)\cup A_{5}
. Note that
N(K)\subseteq N\cup S
. By Proposition
6.1
,
N(K)\setminus S
is a clique. Next we show that
N(K)\setminus S
is complete to
N(K)\cap S
. Suppose by contradiction that
s\in N(K)\cap S
is not adjacent to
t\in N(K)\setminus S
. Let
r\in A_{2}(i+2)
be a neighbor of
s
. If
t\in B_{i}
, then
t-K-s-r-B_{i-2}-B_{i-1}-t
is an induced cyclic sequence. So
t\notin B_{i}
. By Lemma
6.2
,
t\notin A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
. By Lemma
6.3
,
t\notin A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
.
By the choice of
C
,
t\notin A_{5}
. This proves that
N(K)\setminus S
is complete to
N(K)\cap S
. Let
a,b\in N(K)\cap S
be two non-adjacent vertices. If
a,b
have a common neighbor in
K
, then
a,b
have disjoint neighborhoods in
A_{2}(i+2)
which are complete to each other by
G
is
C_{6}
-free. Suppose that
a,b
do not have a common neighbor in
K
. Then each of
a
and
b
mixes on
K
. If
a
mixes on
uv\in K
, then
Since
u-v-a-(N(a)\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}-B_{i}
is not a bad
P_{7}
,
a
is complete to
B_{i}
. By symmetry,
b
is complete to
B_{i}
. So
a,b
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
, which implies that
a,b
have disjoint and complete neighborhoods in
A_{2}(i+2)
. If there is an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
such that
d\in N(K)\cap S
,
c\in K
and
a,b\in(N(K)\cap S)\cup K
, then
a-b-c-d-(N(d)\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
.

Now all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K
where
X=S
and
Y=A_{2}(i+2)
, and so we are done.
∎

Lemma 11.7
.

A_{5}
is complete to
A_{1}
.

Proof.

We show that
A_{5}
is complete to
A_{1}(i)
. Let
S=N(A_{2}(i+2))\cap A_{1}(i)
. By Lemma
11.1
,
A_{5}
is complete to
S
. By Lemma
11.6
, each component of
A_{1}(i)\setminus S
is complete to a pair of non-adjacent vertices in
S
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
A_{5}
is complete to that component.
∎

Lemma 11.8

(Simplicial vertices in
A_{1}(i)
)
.

Let
K
be a component of
A_{1}(i)
and
s
be a
(K,B_{i})
-minimal simplicial vertex.
Then
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+2)
is a clique and so

N(s)\cap A_{2}(i+2)
is a clique of size
>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
complete to
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i-2}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1
applying to
\{s\}
,
N(s)\setminus(K\cup A_{2}(i+2))
is a clique. Next we investigate the neighbor of
s
in
K
and the neighbor of
s
in
N(s)\setminus(A_{2}(i+2)\cup K)\subseteq B_{i}\cup A_{1}(i)\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{3}(i+1)\cup A_{5}
.

By Lemma
6.4
(5),
K
has neighbors in at most one of
A_{3}(i-1)
,
A_{3}(i)
and
A_{3}(i+1)
. If
K
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+1)
, we may assume by Lemma
6.4
(1) that
K
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i)
. So
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+2)\subseteq B_{i}\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{5}\cup K
. If
K
has a neighbor in
A_{3}(i-1)
, then
K
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i)
by Lemmma
6.4
(2). Then
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+2)\subseteq B_{i}\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{5}\cup K
. In either case, we have
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+2)\subseteq B_{i}\cup A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(j)\cup A_{5}\cup K
for
j\in\{i-1,i\}
.

Recall that
N(s)\setminus(A_{2}(i+2)\cup K)
is a clique. If
N(s)\cap B_{i}
is not complete to
N(s)\cap K
, there is an induced
P_{4}
with one end in
B_{i}
and the other three vertices in
A_{1}(i)
by Lemma
2.7
. This contradicts Lemma
6.5
.
So
N(s)\cap B_{i}
is complete to
N(s)\cap K
. By Lemma
6.2
,
N(s)\cap A_{2}(i-1)
is complete to
N(s)\cap K
.
By Lemma
6.3
,
N(s)\cap A_{3}(j)
is complete to
N(s)\cap K
for
j\in\{i-1,i\}
.
By Lemma
11.7
,
N(s)\cap K
is complete to
N(s)\cap A_{5}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Let
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
be the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i-2)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
be the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i+1)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
.

Lemma 11.9
.

Each vertex in
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
is a universal vertex in
A_{2}(i-2)
. By symmetry, each vertex in
A^{1}_{2}(i+1)
is a universal vertex in
A_{2}(i+1)
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
a\in A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
have a non-neighbor
d\in A_{2}(i-2)
. By Lemma
6.6
,
a
is complete to
B_{i-2}
and thus
a
and
d
have a common neighbor
v\in B_{i-2}
.
By definition of
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
a
has a neighbor in a component
K
of
A_{1}(i-1)
. By Lemma
6.2
,
a
is complete to
K
. By Proposition
6.1
,
K
has a vertex
b
that is adjacent to some vertex
c\in A_{2}(i+1)
. Then
d-v-a-b-c-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

11.1
Two non-empty
A_{1}(i)

The global assumption in this subsection is that
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{1}(i+1)
are not empty. By Proposition
6.1
, there is an edge between
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}(i+1)
and there is an edge between
A_{1}(i+1)
and
A_{2}(i-2)
. Let
x\in A_{1}(i-1)
,
y\in A_{1}(i+1)
,
z\in A_{2}(i+1)
and
w\in A_{2}(i-2)
with
xz,yw\in E(G)
. By Lemma
4.9
,
z
is complete to
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
and
w
is complete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
. By Lemma
4.8
,
B_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i-2}
and
B_{i+1}
is complete to
B_{i+2}
. Recall that
\omega=\omega(G)
.

Lemma 11.10
.

Suppose that
x^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i-1)
has a neighbor
z^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i+1)
and
y^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i+1)
has a neighbor
w^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i-2)
. Then exactly one of
x^{\prime}w^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}z^{\prime}
is an edge.

Proof.

By Lemmas
4.15
and
4.18
,
x^{\prime}y^{\prime},z^{\prime}w^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
4.9
,
z^{\prime}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
and
w^{\prime}
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. If
x^{\prime}w^{\prime},y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\notin E(G)
, then
x^{\prime}-N_{B_{i-1}}(x^{\prime})-w^{\prime}-y^{\prime}-N_{B_{i+1}}(y^{\prime})-z^{\prime}-x^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{6}
. If
x^{\prime}w^{\prime},y^{\prime}z^{\prime}\in E(G)
, then
x^{\prime}-w^{\prime}-y^{\prime}-z^{\prime}-x^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
. So exactly one of
x^{\prime}w^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}z^{\prime}
is an edge.
∎

Lemma 11.11
.

If
xw\in E(G)
, then
x
is complete to
B_{i-1}
and
B_{i}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
. By symmetry, if
yz\in E(G)
, then
y
is complete to
B_{i+1}
and
B_{i}
is complete to
B_{i-1}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.10
, exactly one of
xw
and
yz
is an edge. By symmetry, we may assume that
xw\in E(G)
and
yz\notin E(G)
. If
x
has a non-neighbor
s\in B_{i-1}
, then
x-w-s-q_{i}-q_{i+1}-z-x
is an induced
C_{6}
. So
x
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. If
c\in B_{i+1}
is adjacent to
b\in B_{i}
but not adjacent to
a\in B_{i}
, then
a-b-c-(N(c)\cap B_{i+2})-q_{i-2}-w-x
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
B_{i}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
.
∎

Lemma 11.12
.

A_{3}(i-1)\cup A_{3}(i+1)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.10
, we may assume that
xw\in E(G)
.
By Lemma
11.11
,
B_{i+1}
is complete to
B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}
. It follows from Lemma
4.10
that
A_{3}(i+1)=\emptyset
.

Suppose that
A_{3}(i-1)
contains a vertex
t
.
By Lemma
4.10
(1), let
b\in B_{i-1}
be a non-neighbor of
t
.
Since
B_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i-2}
, we can take a common neighbor
c\in B_{i-2}
of
t
and
b
.
Let
d\in N_{B_{i}}(t)
and so
bd\notin E(G)
by Lemma
4.10
(3). By Lemma
11.11
,
xb\in E(G)
.
If
tx\in E(G)
, then
t-c-b-x-t
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
tx\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
4.18
,
tz\notin E(G)
. Then
t-c-b-x-z-q_{i+1}-d-t
is an induced
C_{7}
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.13
.

A_{2}=A_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{2}(i+1)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.10
, we may assume by symmetry that
xw\in E(G)
.
We show that
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i+2)\cup A_{2}(i)=\emptyset
. If
A_{2}(i-1)
contains a vertex
t
, then
x-w-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}-(N(t)\cap B_{i})-t
is a bad
P_{7}
. If
A_{2}(i+2)
contains a vertex
t
, then
t-(N(t)\cap B_{i-2})-w-x-z-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
. This shows that
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i+2)=\emptyset
.

It remains to show that
A_{2}(i)=\emptyset
. Suppose by contradiction that

A_{2}(i)
contains a vertex
a
. If
ay\in E
,
(N(a)\cap B_{i})-a-y-w-x-z-q_{i+2}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
y
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i)
. If
a
has a non-neighbor
b\in B_{i+1}
, then
a-(N(a)\cap B_{i})-b-z-x-w-q_{i-2}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
A_{2}(i)
is complete to
B_{i+1}
.
If
a
is adjacent to some vertex
y^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i+1)\setminus\{y\}
, then
y^{\prime}-a-(N(a)\cap B_{i})-q_{i-1}-x-z-q_{i+2}
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
A_{2}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}=A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{1}(i+1)
. By Lemmas
4.17
,
4.18
,
4.20
,
4.8
,
7.1
and
11.12
, any component of
A_{2}(i)
have a neighbor in
A_{3}(i)
. Let
K
be the component of
A_{2}(i)
containing
a
.

By Lemmas
4.15
,
4.17
and
4.18
,
N(K)\subseteq H\cup A_{3}(i)\cup A_{5}
. Note that any vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{5}
is universal in
N(K)
and
N(K)\cap H
is a clique by Lemma
4.8
. Next we show that
N(K)\cap H
is complete to
N(K)\cap A_{3}(i)
. Suppose first that
a\in A_{2}(i)
is adjacent to
t\in A_{3}(i)
. If
a
has a neighbor
b\in B_{i}\setminus N(t)
, then
b-a-t-N_{B_{i-1}}(t)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-z
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
a
is anticomplete to
B_{i}\setminus N(t)
, i.e.,
N(a)\cap B_{i}\subseteq N(t)\cap B_{i}
. By Lemma
4.8
,
t
is complete to
B_{i+1}
. This shows that
N(K)\cap A_{3}(i)
is complete to
B_{i+1}
. Now suppose that
t\in A_{3}(i)\cap N(K)
is not adjacent to
s\in N(K)\cap B_{i}
. By Lemma
4.10
and the fact that
B_{i}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
,
s
and
t
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-1}
. Let
P
be a shortest path from
s
to
t
such that the internal of
P
is contained in
K
.
Since
N(a)\cap B_{i}\subseteq N(t)\cap B_{i}
,
P
has at least
4
vertices. Then
s-P-t-N_{B_{i-1}}(t)-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}
is an induced path of order at least
7
. This proves that
N(K)\cap H
is complete to
N(K)\cap A_{3}(i)
.
Since
N(K)
is not a clique,
N(K)\cap A_{3}(i)
contains two non-adjacent vertices
t
and
t^{\prime}
. The previous argument shows that
t
and
t^{\prime}
are complete to
B_{i+1}
, and so
t
and
t^{\prime}
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-1}
. Moreover,
t
and
t^{\prime}
have a common neighbor
a\in K
by Lemma
7.2
. If
x
is not adjacent to
t
and
t^{\prime}
, then
t^{\prime}-a-t-(N(t)\cap B_{i-1})-x-z-q_{i+2}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So we may assume by symmetry that
xt\in E
. Then
x-t-q_{i+1}-z-x
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 11.14
.

The following holds for
A_{3}(i)
.

(1)

A_{3}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{1}(i+1)
.

(2)

If
xw\in E(G)
, then
A_{3}(i)
is complete to
B_{i+1}
. By symmetry, if
yz\in E(G)
, then
A_{3}(i)
is complete to
B_{i-1}
.

(3)

Suppose that
A_{3}(i)\neq\emptyset
. If
xw\in E(G)
, then
|B_{i-1}|>\frac{\omega}{2}
. By symmetry, if
yz\in E(G)
, then
|B_{i+1}|>\frac{\omega}{2}
.

Proof.

We prove one by one.

(1) Suppose that
p\in A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{1}(i+1)
is adjacent to
t\in A_{3}(i)
. By Lemma
11.10
, we may assume by symmetry that
xw\in E(G)
and so
B_{i+1}
is complete to
B_{i}
by Lemma
11.11
. By Lemma
4.10
(3),
B_{i}\setminus N(t)
is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}\cap N(t)
. By Lemma
8.1
(1),
q_{i}\in B_{i}\cap N(t)
and
q_{i-1}\in B_{i-1}\setminus N(t)
.
Then
p-t-q_{i}-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-z
is a bad
P_{7}
.

(2) If
t\in A_{3}(i)
is not adjacent to
a\in B_{i+1}
, then
t-(N(t)\cap B_{i})-a-q_{i+2}-q_{i-2}-w-x
is an induced
P_{7}
.

(3) By Lemma
11.13
and (1),
A^{\prime}_{3}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
.
By Lemmas
8.14
and
8.15
, there exist vertices
v\in A_{3}(i)
and
u\in B_{i}\setminus N(v)
such that
|N(v)\cap B_{i-1}|,|N(u)\cap B_{i-1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
Since
B_{i}
is complete to
B_{i+1}
by Lemma
11.11
,
N(v)\cap B_{i-1}
and
N(u)\cap B_{i-1}
are disjoint. So
|B_{i-1}|>\frac{\omega}{2}
.
∎

Recall that
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
is the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i-2)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
is the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i+1)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
.
Let
A_{2}^{0}(i-2)=A_{2}(i-2)\setminus A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
X=A^{0}_{2}(i-2)\cap N(A_{1}(i+1))
and
Y=A^{0}_{2}(i-2)\setminus X
. Let
A_{2}^{0}(i+1)=A_{2}(i+1)\setminus A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
,
X^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\cap N(A_{1}(i-1))
and
Y^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\setminus X^{\prime}
.

Lemma 11.15
.

The following properties hold for
A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i+2)
.

(1)

A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)\cup X
. By symmetry,
A_{3}(i+2)
is complete to
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)\cup X^{\prime}
.

(2)

A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. By symmetry,
A_{3}(i+2)
is complete to
B_{i+1}
.

(3)

A_{3}(i-2)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i-1)
. By symmetry,
A_{3}(i+2)
is complete to
A_{1}(i+1)
.

Proof.

We prove one by one.

(1) Suppose by contradiction that
t\in A_{3}(i-2)
and
a\in A_{2}^{1}(i-2)\cup X
are not adjacent. Suppose first that
a\in A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
. By definition of
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
a
has a neighbor in a component
K
of
A_{1}(i-1)
. By Lemma
6.2
,
a
is complete to
K
. By Proposition
6.1
,
K
has a vertex
b
that is adjacent to some vertex
c\in A_{2}(i+1)
. Then
t-N_{B_{i-2}}(t)-a-b-c-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced sequence. Now suppose that
a\in X
. By definition of
X
,
a
has a neighbor
b\in A_{1}(i+1)
. Then
a-B_{i-1}-t-(N(t)\cap B_{i+2})-(N(b)\cap B_{i+1})-b-a
is an induced cyclic sequence. This proves (1).

(2) By definition,
w\in A_{2}^{1}(i-2)\cup X
. By (1),
w
is complete to
A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
4.8
, (2) holds.

(3)
By Lemma
6.3
,
t
cannot mix on any component
K
of
A_{1}(i-1)
. By Proposition
6.1
,
K
has a neighbor
p^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i+1)
. Let
p\in K
be a neighbor of
p^{\prime}
. If
tp\in E(G)
, then
t-p-p^{\prime}-N_{B_{i+2}}(t)-t
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
t
is not adjacent to
p
and thus anticomplete to
K
.
∎

Lemma 11.16
.

G
has no induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
contained in
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
such that
a
has a neighbor in
B_{i+2}
that is not a neighbor of
b
. By symmetry,
G
has no induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
contained in
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{3}(i+2)
such that
a
has a neighbor in
B_{i-2}
that is not a neighbor of
b
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
a^{\prime}\in B_{i+2}
be a neighbor of
a
not adjacent to
b
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
a^{\prime}c\notin E(G)
.
Note that
a\in B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
. If
d\in B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
, then
a^{\prime}d\notin E(G)
since
B_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
by Lemma
11.15
(2).
If
d\in A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}
, then
a^{\prime}d\notin E(G)
by definition.
Then
d-c-b-a-a^{\prime}-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.17
.

Each vertex in
B_{i-1}
is either complete or anticomplete to any component
K
of
A_{1}(i-1)
. By symmetry, each vertex in
B_{i+1}
is either complete or anticomplete to any component of
A_{1}(i+1)
.

Proof.

If
c\in B_{i-1}
mixes on an edge
ab\in K
, then
a-b-c-(N(c)\cap B_{i-2})-q_{i+2}-(N(y)\cap B_{i+1})-y
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.18
.

Let
K
be a component of
A_{1}(i-1)
. Then any simplicial
s
vertex of
K
has
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+1)
is a clique and so

|N(s)\cap A_{2}(i+1)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
By symmetry, any simplicial
s
vertex of
A_{1}(i+1)
has
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i-2)
is a clique and so
|N(s)\cap A_{2}(i-2)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1
applying to
\{s\}
,
N(s)\setminus(K\cup A_{2}(i+1))
is a clique. Next, we investigate the neighbor of
s
in
K
and the neighbor of
s
in
N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+1)
.
By Lemma
11.15
(3) and Lemma
11.14
(1),

N(s)\setminus A_{2}(i+1)\subseteq B_{i-1}\cup A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{5}\cup K
.
By Lemma
6.2
,
N(s)\cap K
is complete to
N(s)\cap A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
11.17
,

N(s)\cap K
is complete to
N(s)\cap B_{i-1}
. By Lemma
11.7
,
N(s)\cap K
is complete to
N(s)\cap A_{5}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 11.19
.

A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
is chordal. By symmetry,
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{3}(i+2)
is chordal.

Proof.

We first show that
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
is chordal. Since
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
is a clique, any induced
C_{5}
contains at most two vertices from
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
and so any induced
C_{5}
in
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
contains an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
where
a,b,c\in A_{2}(i-2)
and
d\in B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
by Lemma
11.4
. This contradicts Lemma
7.3
. So
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
is chordal.

Suppose that
A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
contains an induced
C_{5}=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}
.
Suppose first that
v_{1}\in A_{2}(i-2)
. If
v_{2}\in B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
, then
v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}-v_{2}-N_{B_{i+2}}(v_{2})-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
v_{2},v_{5}\in A_{2}(i-2)
. By the same argument,
v_{3},v_{4}\in A_{2}(i-2)
. This contradicts Lemma
11.4
. So we may assume that
C_{5}
is contained in
B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
. Since
B_{i-2}
is a clique and
A_{3}(i-2)
is
P_{4}
-free, we may assume that
v_{1},v_{2}\in B_{i-2}
and
v_{3},v_{4},v_{5}\in A_{3}(i-2)
. But this contradicts Lemma
8.3
. So
A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
is chordal.

If
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
contains an induced
C_{5}=v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{3}-v_{4}-v_{5}
, then we may assume that
v_{1}\in B_{i-1}
and
v_{2}\in A_{3}(i-2)
. But then
v_{4}-v_{5}-v_{1}-v_{2}-N_{B_{i+2}}(v_{2})-N_{B_{i+1}}(y)-y
is a bad
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.20
.

If
A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
, then
|B_{i-1}|,|B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Let
b\in B_{i}
such that
|N(b)\cap(B_{i-1}\cup B_{i+1})|
is minimum.
By Lemma
11.12
and the assumption,
A_{3}(j)=\emptyset
for
j\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}
.
Then
N(b)=(N(b)\cap(B_{i}\cup B_{i-1}))\cup B_{i+1}
.
By the choice of
b
,
N(b)\cap(B_{i}\cup B_{i-1})
is a clique.
So
d(b)\leq(\omega-1)+|N(b)\cap B_{i+1}|
.
Since
G
has no small vertices,
|N(b)\cap B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
∎

Lemma 11.21
.

A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{5}
is a clique. By symmetry,
A^{1}_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5}
is a clique.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.6
,
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
is complete to
B_{i-2}
. Suppose that
p\in A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
is not adjacent to
q\in B_{i-1}\cup A_{5}
. Suppose first that
q\in A_{5}
. By definition of
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
p
is complete to a component
K
of
A_{1}(i-1)
. By Proposition
6.1
,
K
has a vertex
a
that has a neighbor in
A_{2}(i+1)
. By the choice of
C=C_{5}
,
qa\in E(G)
. Then
p-(N(p)\cap B_{i-2})-q-a-p
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
q\in B_{i-1}
. If
q
is complete to
K
, then
q-(N(p)\cap K)-p-(N(q)\cap B_{i-2})-q
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
q
is anticomplete to
K
, then
q-q_{i-2}-p-a-(N(a)\cap A_{2}(i+1))-q_{i+1}-q_{i}-q
is an induced
C_{7}
.
By Lemma
11.9
and the fact that
B_{i-2}
is complete to
B_{i-1}
,
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{5}
is a clique.
∎

Lemma 11.22
.

If each vertex in
A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
, then any simplicial vertex of
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
is not in
Y
. By symmetry, if each vertex in
A_{2}(i+1)\setminus Y^{\prime}
is universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
, then any simplicial vertex of
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{3}(i+2)
is not in
Y^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
S=A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
.
Suppose by contradiction that there is a simplicial vertex
u
of
S
in
Y
.
Let
K
be the component of
Y
containing
u
.
We choose a vertex
s\in N_{S}[u]\cap K
simplicial in
S
such that
|N(s)\cap A_{5}|
is minimum.

By definition of
Y
,
K
is anticomplete to
A_{1}
.
By Lemma
11.12
,
N(K)\subseteq A_{5}\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup(A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y)\cup A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.21
,
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{5}
is a clique. By the choice of
C
,
X
is complete
A_{5}
. By Lemma
4.9
,
X
is complete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}
. By the assumption that each vertex in
A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
,
N(K)\setminus A_{3}(i-2)
is a clique.
By Lemma
11.15
(1) and (2),
A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
B_{i-1}\cup(A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y)\cup A_{5}
. It follows that each vertex in
N(K)\setminus A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
is universal in
N(K)
.
By Lemma
11.15
(2), any two non-adjacent vertices in
N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
has disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
.
If there is an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
a,b\in K\cup(N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2))
,
c\in K
and
d\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2))
, then
a-b-c-d-N_{B_{i+2}}(d)-q_{i+1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
.
So all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K
with
X=A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
and
Y=B_{i+2}
, and so
K
is clique.

We claim that
s
is simplicial in
G
. Suppose not.
Since
s
is simplicial in
S
, there exist vertices
p\in N(s)\cap Y
and
q\in A_{5}
that are not adjacent by Lemmas
11.21
and the choice of
C
.
By the choice of
s
and
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
p
is not simplicial in
S
and so has a neighbor
a\in S\setminus N(s)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
aq\notin E(G)
. Since
q\in A_{5}
is complete to
S\setminus Y
, we have
a\in Y
. Now the induced path
s-p-a
contradicts that
K
is a clique.
∎

Lemma 11.23
.

If
u\in A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}
is simplicial in
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
, then
N(u)\setminus(B_{i+2}\cup A_{3}(i+2))
is a clique and so
|N(u)\cap(B_{i+2}\cup A_{3}(i+2))|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
By symmetry, if
u\in A_{3}(i+2)\cup B_{i+2}
is simplicial in
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{3}(i+2)
. Then
N(u)\setminus(B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2))
is a clique and so
|N(u)\cap(B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2))|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Let
S=A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
.
Suppose that
N(u)\setminus(B_{i+2}\cup A_{3}(i+2))
is not a clique.
Since
u
is simplicial in
S
, there exist vertices
p\in Y
and
q\in A_{5}
with
pq\notin E(G)
.
Let
K
be the component of
Y\setminus N(q)
containing
p
. Since
N(K)
is not a clique,
N(K)
contains two non-adjacent vertices
a,b
. Note that
a,b\in A_{5}\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)\cup(A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y)\cup(N(q)\cap Y)
are adjacent to
q
. So
a
and
b
do not have a common neighbor in
K
.
We show that if
a\notin A_{5}
, then
au\in E(G)
. Suppose not. Since
q
is adjacent to both
u
and
a
,
u
and
a
have no common neighbors in
K
.
Then there exists an induced path
P=a-\cdots-u
with at least 4 vertices such that
P\setminus\{a,u\}\subseteq K
. This contradicts Lemma
11.16
.
If
a,b\notin A_{5}
,
u
is not adjacent to at least one of
a,b
since
u
is simplicial in
S
, a contradiction. Therefore,
a\in A_{5}
and
b\in Y
with
bu\in E(G)
. Since
a,b
have no common neighbors in
K
, there exist
c,d\in K
such that
a-c-d-b
is an induced
P_{4}
and so
a-c-d-b-q-a
is an induced
C_{5}
. Since
u
is simplicial in
S
,
u
is not adjacent to
c
. Then
u
is not adjacent to
d
for otherwise
a-c-d-u-a
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
c-d-b-u
contradicts Lemma
11.16
.
∎

Next, we consider two cases. The first case is that some vertex in
X
is not universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
or some vertex in
X^{\prime}
is not universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
, and the second case is that every vertex in
X
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
and every vertex in
X^{\prime}
is universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
.

11.1.1
X
or
X^{\prime}
is not universal

By symmetry, we may assume that
a\in X^{\prime}
is not universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
. By definition of
X^{\prime}
,
a
has a neighbor
b\in A_{1}(i-1)
and
ay\notin E(G)
. By applying Lemma
11.10
to edges
ab
and
yw
, we have
bw\in E(G)
(at this point, we break the symmetry). For consistency, we rename
a
as
z
and rename
b
as
x
. So the global assumption in this subsection is that
z
has a non-neighbor
z^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i+1)
. It follows from Lemma
11.11
that
G
contains the graph in Figure
5
as an induced subgraph.

B_{i}
B_{i-1}
B_{i+1}
B_{i-2}
B_{i+2}
x
w
y
z

Figure 5
:
A basic graph arising from two non-empty
A_{1}(j)
s. A double line means complete.

Lemma 11.24
.

A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

If
a\in B_{i-1}
is adjacent to
b\in B_{i}
but not adjacent to
c\in B_{i}
, then
z^{\prime}-(N(z^{\prime})\cap B_{i+2})-z-x-a-b-c
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
B_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i}
. By Lemma
11.11
,
B_{i+1}
is complete to
B_{i}
. By Lemma
4.10
(3),
A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma 11.25
.

A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
A_{3}(i+2)
contains a vertex
t
. Since
B_{i+1}
is complete to
B_{i+2}
,
B_{i+2}\setminus N(t)
is anticomplete to
N(t)\cap B_{i-2}
by Lemma
4.10
. By Lemmas
4.16
and
4.20
,
tx,tw\notin E(G)
.

Case 1.
z^{\prime}
has a neighbor
b
in
B_{i+2}\setminus N(t)
.

If
tz\notin E(G)
, then
z^{\prime}-b-z-x-w-(N(t)\cap B_{i-2})-t
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
tz\in E(G)
. If
tz^{\prime}\in E(G)
, then
z^{\prime}-b-t-z^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
tz^{\prime}\notin E(G)
and thus
z^{\prime}-b-z-t-(N(t)\cap B_{i-2})-q_{i-1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
.

Case 2.
z^{\prime}
has no neighbors in
B_{i+2}\setminus N(t)
.

By
Case 1
,
A_{2}(i+1)\setminus N(z)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i-1)
.
By Lemma
11.9
,
A_{2}(i+1)\setminus N(z)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}
.
Let
K_{z^{\prime}}
be the component of
A_{2}(i+1)\setminus N(z)
containing
z^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
4.18
,
K_{z^{\prime}}
is anticomplete to
A_{2}\setminus N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
. By Lemmas
4.18
,
4.20
,
11.12
and
11.24
,
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A_{3}\subseteq A_{3}(i+2)
and so
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\subseteq B_{i+1}\cup N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)\cup A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)\cup A_{5}
.

Next we show that
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A_{5}
is complete to
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
. For contradiction, let
p\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
and
q\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A_{5}
be non-adjacent. By the choice of
C
,
q
is complete to
\{x,z\}
. If
p
and
q
have a common neighbor
c\in K_{z^{\prime}}
, then
c-p-z-q-c
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
p
and
q
have disjoint neighborhoods in
K_{z^{\prime}}
. This implies that
p
mixes on an edge
ab\in K_{z^{\prime}}
. Then
xp\in E(G)
for otherwise
a-b-p-z-x-q_{i-1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
. By Lemma
4.9
,
p
is complete to
B_{i+2}
. Then
p-x-q-q_{i+2}-p
is an induced
C_{4}
.
By Lemma
4.14
, each vertex in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A_{5}
is universal in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})
.

Next we show that
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap B_{i+1}
is complete to
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
. For contradiction, let
p\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
and
q\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap B_{i+1}
be non-adjacent. By definition,
pz\in E(G)
and
qz\in E(G)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
p
and
q
do not have a common neighbor in
K_{z^{\prime}}
. So
p
mixes on
K_{z^{\prime}}
and thus
xp\in E(G)
. By Lemma
4.9
,
pq\in E(G)
, a contradiction. By Lemma
4.8
, each vertex in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap B_{i+1}
is universal in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})
.

We show that
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
is a clique complete to
A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
. Suppose that
p,q\in N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
are not adjacent. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
p
and
q
have disjoint neighborhoods in
K_{z^{\prime}}
. This implies that both
p
and
q
mix on
K_{z^{\prime}}
and so
xp,xq\in E(G)
. Then
p-x-q-q_{i+2}-p
is an induced
C_{4}
. This shows that
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
is a clique. Suppose that
p\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
and
q\in N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
be non-adjacent. Recall that
qx\notin E(G)
. If
qz\notin E(G)
, then
q-K_{z^{\prime}}-\{p,z\}-x-w-B_{i-2}-q
is an induced cyclic sequence. So
qz\in E(G)
. Since
pz\in E(G)
and
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
p
and
q
do not have common neighbors in
K_{z^{\prime}}
. So
p
mixes on
K_{z^{\prime}}
and so
xp\in E(G)
. Then
q-K_{z^{\prime}}-p-x-w-B_{i-2}-q
is an induced cyclic sequence. So each vertex in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap N_{A_{2}(i+1)}(z)
is universal in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})
.

Since
B_{i+1}
is complete to
A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
by Lemma
11.15
(2), each pair of non-adjacent vertices in
N(K_{z^{\prime}})\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
has disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-2}
. If there is an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
a,b\in K_{z^{\prime}}\cup N(K_{z^{\prime}})
,
c\in K_{z^{\prime}}
and
d\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
, then
a-b-c-d-N_{B_{i-2}}(d)-q_{i-1}-q_{i}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K_{z^{\prime}}
with
X=A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
and
Y=B_{i-2}
. It follows that
K_{z^{\prime}}
is complete to two non-adjacent vertices
p,q\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i+2)
. If
zq\notin E(G)
, then
p-z^{\prime}-q-(N(q)\cap B_{i-2})-w-x-z
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
zq\in E(G)
. By symmetry,
zp\in E(G)
. Then
p-z^{\prime}-q-z-p
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 11.26
.

Each vertex in
A_{1}(i+1)
has a neighbor in
A_{2}(i-2)
.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq A_{1}(i+1)
be the set of vertices that have a neighbor in
A_{2}(i-2)
and
T=A_{1}(i+1)\setminus S
. The lemma is equivalent to show

T=\emptyset
. Suppose not. Let
K
be a connected component of
T
.
By Lemmas
11.24
and
11.25
,

N(K)=(N(K)\cap B_{i+1})\cup(N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1))\cup(N(K)\cap A_{2}(i+1))\cup(N(K)\cap A_{5}).

By Proposition
6.1
,
N(K)\setminus A_{1}(i+1)
is a clique.
Observe that any vertex
p\in S
is not adjacent to
z
by Lemma
11.9
and the assumption that
z
is not universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
. If a vertex
p\in N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1)
is mixed on
ab\in K
, then
a-b-p-(N(p)\cap A_{2}(i-2))-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-z
is a bad
P_{7}
. So any vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1)
is complete to
K
.

We show that
N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1)
is complete to
N(K)\setminus A_{1}(i+1)
. Suppose by contradiction that
p\in N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1)
and
q\in N(K)\setminus A_{1}(i+1)
with
pq\notin E(G)
.
By definition of
p
,
p
has a neighbor
w^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i-2)
.
Let
u\in K
be a common neighbor of
p
and
q
.
By Lemma
11.7
,
q\notin A_{5}
.
If
q\in B_{i+1}
, then
p-u-q-q_{i}-q_{i-1}-w^{\prime}-p
is an induced
C_{6}
.
If
q\in A_{2}(i+1)
, then
p-u-q-N_{B_{i+2}}(q)-q_{i-2}-w^{\prime}-p
is an induced
C_{6}
.

Therefore, each vertex in
N(K)\setminus A_{1}(i+1)
is universal in
N(K)
. Since
N(K)
is not clique, there are two non-adjacent vertices
p,q\in N(K)\cap A_{1}(i+1)
. So
p
and
q
have a common neighbor
u\in K
and thus
p
and
q
have disjoint neighborhoods in
A_{2}(i-2)
. Since
pz\notin E(G)
,
q-u-p-(N(p)\cap A_{2}(i-2))-q_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-z
is a bad
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.27

(
P_{4}
-freeness of
A_{1}(i+1)
)
.

The following properties hold for
A_{1}(i+1)
.

(1)

x
is complete to
N(A_{1}(i+1))\cap A_{2}(i-2)
.

(2)

For any vertex
w^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i-2)
and any component
K
of
A_{1}(i+1)
,
w
is either complete or anticomplete to
K
.

(3)

A_{1}(i+1)
is
P_{4}
-free.

Proof.

We prove these properties one by one.

(1) Let
w^{\prime}\in N(A_{1}(i+1))\cap A_{2}(i-2)
and
y^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i+1)
be a neighbor of
w^{\prime}
. By Lemma
11.9
,
zy^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
11.10
applying to
xz
and
y^{\prime}w^{\prime}
,
xw^{\prime}\in E(G)
.

(2) Suppose by contradiction that there exists an edge
ab\in E(K)
such that
w^{\prime}
is adjacent to
b
but not to
a
. By (1),
xw^{\prime}\in E(G)
and thus
a-b-w^{\prime}-x-z-(N(z^{\prime})\cap B_{i+2})-z^{\prime}
is a bad
P_{7}
.

(3) It suffices to show that each component
K
of
A_{1}(i+1)
is
P_{4}
-free.
By Lemma
11.26
and (2), there exists a vertex
w^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i-2)
that is complete to
K
.
If there is an induced
P_{5}=a-b-c-d-e
with
a,b,c,d\in K
and
e\in B_{i+1}
, then
a-b-c-d-e-q_{i}-q_{i-1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. By Lemma
2.8
,
K
is
P_{4}
-free.
∎

Lemma 11.28
.

A^{0}_{2}(i-2)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i+1)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
a\in A^{0}_{2}(i-2)
is adjacent to
b\in A_{1}(i+1)
.
By definition of
A^{0}_{2}(i-2)
,
ax\notin E(G)
. Applying Lemma
11.10
to the edges
xz
and
ab
, we have
bz\in E(G)
.
By Lemma
11.9
,
z
is universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
. This contradicts the assumption that
z
is not universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
.
∎

Lemma 11.29
.

A_{2}^{0}(i-2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Let
L
be a component of
A_{2}^{0}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.27
(1),
L
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i+1)
. By Lemma
11.28
,
N(L)\subseteq A_{5}\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.21
,
N(L)\setminus A_{3}(i-2)
is a clique.
Since
G
has no clique cutsets,
L
has neighbors in
A_{3}(i-2)
and so
A_{3}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
.

Let
S=B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
.
Since
A_{3}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
,
S
is not a clique and so
S
has two non-adjacent simplicial vertices
u,u^{\prime}
by Lemma
11.19
.
Since
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}
is a clique, we may assume that
u\in A^{0}_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
11.28
,
X=\emptyset
and so
A_{2}(i-2)\setminus Y=A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.9
, each vertex in
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
11.22
,
u\in A_{3}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
11.15
(1) and (2) and Lemma
11.22
,
u^{\prime}\in A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}
.
By Lemma
11.25
,
A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
.
It follows from Lemma
11.23
that
|N(u)\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
and
|N(u^{\prime})\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. Since
A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}
is complete to
B_{i-1}
,
u
and
u^{\prime}
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
. By Lemma
11.18
,
|A_{2}(i+1)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. By Lemmas
11.24
and Lemma
11.20
,
|B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
So
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{2}(i+1)
contains a clique of size larger than
\omega
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.30
.

There is a vertex
b\in B_{i-2}
anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)
such that
|N(b)\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
This implies that
A_{3}(i-2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.13
, there is a vertex
b\in B_{i-2}
that is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemmas
11.21
and
11.29
, we have
N(b)\setminus B_{i+2}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|N(b)\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

By Lemma
11.18
,
A_{2}(i+1)
contains a clique
M
of size larger than
\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
complete to
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. Suppose that
A_{3}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
8.5
, let
t
be a simplicial vertex of
A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
8.3
,
N(t)\cap A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
N(t)\cap B_{i-2}
. By Lemma
11.15
(1), (2) and Lemma
11.21
,
N(t)\setminus B_{i+2}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|N(t)\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. Since
N(t)\cap B_{i+2}
and
N(b)\cap B_{i+2}
are disjoint,
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup M
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.31
.

A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique.

Proof.

Let
K
be the component of
A_{1}(i-1)
containing
x
. By Lemmas
11.11
and
11.17
,
K
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. If there is simplicial vertex of
A_{1}(i-1)
not adjacent to some simplicial vertex of
K
, then
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
contains a clique larger than
\omega
by Lemmas
11.18
and
11.30
. This implies that
A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique.
∎

Lemma 11.32
.

A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i-1)
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
v\in A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
has a neighbor
v^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i-1)
. By definition,
v
has a neighbor
a\in A_{1}(i+1)
. By Lemma
11.26
,
a
has a neighbor
a^{\prime}\in A_{2}(i-2)
. By applying Lemma
11.10
to
aa^{\prime}
and
vv^{\prime}
,
a^{\prime}v^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. By Lemma
11.27
(3),
xa^{\prime}\in E(G)
. Since
A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique by Lemma
11.31
,
a^{\prime}
mixes on
xv^{\prime}
which contradicts Lemma
6.2
.
∎

Recall that
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
is the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i+1)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
,
A_{2}^{0}(i+1)=A_{2}(i+1)\setminus A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
,
X^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\cap N(A_{1}(i-1))
and
Y^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\setminus X^{\prime}
.

Lemma 11.33
.

Let
s\in A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
be a simplicial vertex of
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. If
s\in X^{\prime}
, then
N(s)\setminus A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique and so
|N(s)\cap A_{1}(i-1)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Suppose not.
By Lemma
11.32
,
s
is anticomplete to
A_{1}(i+1)
and thus
N(s)\setminus A_{1}(i-1)\subseteq B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{2}(i+1)\cup A_{5}
.
By Lemma
11.21
and
11.1
, there exist vertices
b\in N(s)\cap Y^{\prime}
and
q\in N(s)\cap A_{5}
that are not adjacent.

Let
K
be the component of
Y^{\prime}\setminus N(q)
containing
b
.
Note that
N(K)\subseteq A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5}
. By Lemmas
11.9
and
11.21
,

N(K)\cap(A_{2}^{1}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5})
is a clique and complete to
N(K)\cap X^{\prime}
.
Since
N(K)
is not a clique,
K
has two non-adjacent neighbors
p_{1},p_{2}
. Suppose first that
p_{1}\in X^{\prime}
. Then
p_{2}\in X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
.
If
p_{1},p_{2}
have a common neighbor
u\in K
, then
p_{1}-u-p_{2}-q-p_{1}
is an induced
C_{4}
. So
p_{1},p_{2}
have no common neighbors in
K
.
Let
P
be a shortest path from
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
whose internal is contained in
K
. So
|P|\geq 4
. Then
p_{2}-P-p_{1}-r-N_{B_{i-1}}(r)-q_{i}
is an induced path with at least 7 vertices, where
r
is a neighbor of
p_{1}\in A_{1}(i-1)
.
This proves that each vertex in
N(K)\cap X^{\prime}
is universal in
N(K)
.
So
p_{1},p_{2}\notin X^{\prime}
and
s
is adjacent to
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.

Therefore, we may assume that
p_{1}\in Y^{\prime}
. Since
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
is universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
,
p_{2}\in Y^{\prime}\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5}
.
Since
s
is simplicial in
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
, we have
p_{2}\in A_{5}
.
Since
q
is a common neighbor of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
,
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have no common neighbors in
K
.
Since
s
is simplicial in
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
,
N(s)\cap K\subseteq N(p_{1})\cap K
and so
s
and
p_{2}
have no common neighbors in
K
. Let
P=s-v_{1}-\cdots v_{k}-p_{2}
be a shortest path between
s
and
p_{2}
with
v_{i}\in K
for all
i\in[k]
. If
k=2
, then
s-v_{1}-v_{2}-p_{2}-s
is an induced
C_{4}
. If
k\geq 3
, then
v_{3}-v_{2}-v_{1}-s-r-N_{B_{i-1}}(r)-q_{i}
is an induced path at least 7 vertices, where
r\in A_{1}(i-1)
is a neighbor of
s
.
∎

Lemma 11.34
.

\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.18
,
A_{2}(i+1)
contains a clique
M
complete to
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
with
|M|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
Since
A_{5}\cup M\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
is a clique,
|A_{5}|<\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

(1)
A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique and complete to
B_{i-1}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
.

By Lemma
6.2
and
A_{2}(i-2)=A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
A_{2}(i-2)
is complete to
A_{1}(i-1)
. By
x
is complete to
B_{i-1}
and Lemma
11.17
,
A_{1}(i-1)
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. This proves (1).

Let
s\in A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
be a simplicial vertex in
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
. If
s\in Y^{\prime}
, then
N(s)\setminus A_{5}
is a clique and so
|A_{5}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
, a contradiction.
So
s\notin Y^{\prime}
.

(2)
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
is a clique.

Suppose not.
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
contains two non-adjacent simplicial vertices
s,s^{\prime}
by Lemma
11.19
.
Since
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
is a clique complete to
X^{\prime}
, we have
s,s^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
11.33
,
|N(s)\cap A_{1}(i-1)|,|N(s^{\prime})\cap A_{1}(i-1)|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. Since
N(s)\cap A_{1}(i-1)
and
N(s^{\prime})\cap A_{1}(i-1)
are disjoint and complete, then
B_{i-1}\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
by (1). This proves (2).

By (2),
Y^{\prime}=\emptyset
. By Lemmas
11.1
,
11.21
and
11.7
, each vertex in
A_{5}
is universal in
G
. Since
G
is basic,
A_{5}=\emptyset
.

(3)
A_{1}(i+1)
is a clique.

Suppose not. Let
s,s^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i+1)
be two non-adjacent simplicial vertices of
A_{1}(i+1)
.
If
N(s)\cap A_{2}(i-2)
and
N(s^{\prime})\cap A_{2}(i-2)
are disjoint, then
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
by Lemmas
11.18
,
11.33
and
11.20
. So
s
and
s^{\prime}
have a common neighbor in
A_{2}(i-2)
and so
s
and
s^{\prime}
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
.
By Lemma
11.27
(2) and
A_{2}^{0}(i-2)=\emptyset
,
|N(s)\cap(B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1))|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
and
|N(s^{\prime})\cap(B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1))|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. Then
M\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
. This proves (3).

(4) For every vertex
v\in A_{1}(i+1)
,
|N(v)\cap B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

If
v
has no neighbors in
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
, then
N(v)\cap B_{i+1}=N(v)\cap(B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1))
and so the statement holds since
|N(v)\cap(B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}^{1}(i+1))|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
So we may assume that
v
has a neighbor
c\in A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
.
If
v
has a non-neighbor
v^{\prime}\in B_{i+1}
, then
v^{\prime}-c-v-N_{A_{2}(i-2)}(v)-q_{i-1}-q_{i}-v^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{6}
.
Then
v
is complete to
B_{i+1}
and so
|N(v)\cap B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
This proves (4).

Next we give a
\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil
-coloring of
G
.
Let
x=|B_{i-1}|
,
y=|B_{i-2}|
, and
z=|A_{2}(i-2)|
.
Our strategy is to use
p=x+y+z-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
colors to color some vertices of
G
first and then argue that the remaining vertices can be colored with
f=\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil-p
colors.
Note that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil-(x+y+z-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil)

\displaystyle=\omega+(4\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil-(x+y+z))

\displaystyle\geq\omega,

since
x+y+z\leq\omega
.

Step 1: Precolor a subgraph.

Next we define vertices to be colored with
p
colors. Let
S_{i-1}\subseteq B_{i-1}
be the set of
x-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices,
S_{i-2}\subseteq B_{i-2}
be the set of
y-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices,
S_{z}\subseteq A_{2}(i-2)
be the set of
z-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices, and
S_{i+1}\subseteq B_{i+1}
be the set of
p
vertices with largest neighborhoods in
A_{1}(i+1)
. Since
(x+y+z)-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil<|B_{i+1}|
, the choice of
S_{i}
is possible.
By (4),
S_{i+1}
is complete to
A_{1}(i+1)
.
We color
S_{i+1}
with all colors in
[p]
, and color all vertices in
S_{i-1}\cup S_{i-2}\cup S_{z}
with all colors in
[p]
. Since
B_{i-1}
is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
, this coloring is proper. So it suffices to show that

G_{1}=G-(S_{i-1}\cup S_{i-2}\cup S_{i+1}\cup S_{z})
is
f
-colorable.

Step 2: Reduce by degeneracy.

Let
v
be a vertex in
B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1)
.
Note that
|N_{G_{1}}(v)|<\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil+(\omega-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil)\leq f
.
It follows that
G_{1}
is
f
-colorable if and only if
G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1))
is
f
-colorable.
Since
G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1))
is chordal,
\chi(G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1)))\leq\omega\leq f
.
∎

11.1.2
X
and
X^{\prime}
are universal

Recall that
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
is the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i-2)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}^{0}(i-2)=A_{2}(i-2)\setminus A_{2}^{1}(i-2)
,
X=A^{0}_{2}(i-2)\cap N(A_{1}(i+1))
and
Y=A^{0}_{2}(i-2)\setminus X
. Similarly.
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
is the set of vertices in
A_{2}(i+1)
that have a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
and
A_{2}^{0}(i+1)=A_{2}(i+1)\setminus A_{2}^{1}(i+1)
, and
X^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\cap N(A_{1}(i-1))
and
Y^{\prime}=A^{0}_{2}(i+1)\setminus X^{\prime}
. The global assumption in this subsection is that each vertex in
X
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
and each vertex in
X^{\prime}
is universal in
A_{2}(i+1)
.

Lemma 11.35
.

A_{3}(i-2)=A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose by symmetry that
A_{3}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
11.12
,
A_{3}(i-1)=\emptyset
. Since
A_{3}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
,
S=A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{3}(i-2)
is not clique. By Lemma
11.19
,
S
contains two non-adjacent simplicial vertices
u
and
u^{\prime}
. Since each vertex in
X
is universal in
A_{2}(i-2)
,
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup X\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}
is a clique. So we may assume that
u\in Y\cup A_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.22
,
u\in A_{3}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
11.15
(1) and (2) and Lemma
11.22
,
u^{\prime}\in A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}
.
Since
u\in A_{3}(i-2)
and
uu^{\prime}\notin E(G)
,
N(u)\cap A_{3}^{\prime}(i+2)\subseteq B_{i+2}
and
N(u^{\prime})\cap A_{3}^{\prime}(i+2)\subseteq B_{i+2}
by Lemma
8.11
.
By Lemma
11.23
,
|N_{B_{i+2}}(u)|,|N_{B_{i+2}}(u^{\prime})|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Since
A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}
is complete to
B_{i-1}
,
u
and
u^{\prime}
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
. By Lemma
11.18
,
|X^{\prime}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
If
A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
, then
|B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
by Lemma
11.20
.
If
A_{3}(i)\neq\emptyset
, then
|B_{i+1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
by Lemmas
8.15
and
11.13
.
So
B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{2}(i+1)
contains a clique of size larger than
\omega
, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 11.36
.

Y=Y^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

If
Y\neq\emptyset
, then
N(Y)\subseteq A^{1}_{2}(i-2)\cup X\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{5}
by Lemmas
11.12
and
11.35
, and so is a clique. This contradicts that
G
has no clique cutsets.
∎

Lemma 11.37
.

A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose that
t\in A_{3}(i)
.
If
a\in A_{1}(i+1)
is adjacent to
b\in A_{2}(i+1)
, then
t-q_{i}-q_{i-1}-q_{i-2}-(N(b)\cap B_{i+2})-b-a
is an induced
P_{7}
.
Then
A_{1}(i+1)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+1)
and so
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)=\emptyset
.
By Lemma
11.35
,
A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
.
By Lemma
11.36
,
Y^{\prime}=\emptyset
. By Lemma
11.15
(2) and Lemma
8.1
(1),
q_{i+2}
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)
. It follows that
N(q_{i+2})\setminus B_{i-2}\subseteq B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup X^{\prime}\cup A_{5}
is a clique. Since
G
has no small vertices,
|B_{i-2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
By Lemma
11.18
,
A_{2}^{1}(i-2)\cup X
contains a clique
L
of size larger than
\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
By Lemma
11.14
(3), then
|B_{i-1}|>\frac{\omega}{2}
. By Lemmas
11.15
(1) and (2),
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup L
contains a clique of size larger than
\omega
, a contradiction.
∎

We say that an edge
ab\in E(G)
with
a\in A_{1}(i-1)
and
b\in A_{2}(i-2)
is a
vertical edge
if
a
has a neighbor in
A_{2}(i+1)
and
b
has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
. A vertical edge between
A_{1}(i+1)
and
A_{2}(i+1)
is defined in a similar way.

Lemma 11.38
.

Vertical edges between
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}(i-2)
and vertical edges between
A_{1}(i+1)
and
A_{2}(i+1)
cannot exist at the same time.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Without loss of generality,
xw\in E(G)
and
zy\notin E(G)
.
By Lemmas
11.37
,
11.12
and
11.35
,
A_{3}=\emptyset
.
By Lemma
11.36
,
A_{2}(i-2)\cup B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{5}
is a clique and
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5}
is a clique.
Since
q_{i-2}
is not a small vertex,
|B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. By symmetry,
|B_{i-2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Next we show that
A_{1}(j)
is a clique for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
. Let
K
be the component of
A_{1}(i-1)
containing
x
. By Lemmas
11.11
and
11.17
,
K
is complete to
B_{i-1}
. If there is simplicial vertex of
A_{1}(i-1)
not adjacent to some simplicial vertex of
K
, then
A_{2}(i+1)\cup B_{i+1}\cup B_{i+2}
contains a clique larger than
\omega
by Lemma
11.18
. This implies that
A_{1}(i-1)
is a clique. By symmetry
A_{1}(i+1)
is a clique.

Suppose that
v\in A_{1}(i+1)
has a neighbor
a\in A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
and a neighbor
b\in X
. Let
c\in A_{1}(i-1)
be a neighbor of
a
that has a neighbor
d\in A_{2}(i+1)
. By Lemma
11.10
,
v
is adjacent to and non-adjacent to
d
, a contradiction. So no vertex in
A_{1}(i+1)
can have a neighbor in both
A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
and
X
.
Next we show that
X=\emptyset
. Suppose not. Let
a\in X
and
b\in A_{1}(i+1)
be a neighbor of
a
. Then
b\neq y
and
bw\notin E(G)
. Since
A_{1}(i+1)
is a clique,
b-y-w-a-b
is an induced
C_{4}
.
This proves that
X=\emptyset
. So
A_{2}(i-2)=A^{1}_{2}(i-2)
or equivalently each vertex in
A_{2}(i-2)
has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i-1)
. By symmetry, each vertex in
A_{2}(i+1)
has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i+1)
.

By Lemmas
6.2
,
A_{2}(i-2)
is complete to
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{2}(i+1)
is complete to
A_{1}(i+1)
. This contradicts that
zy\notin E(G)
.
∎

Lemma 11.39
.

\chi(G)\leq\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
11.37
,
11.12
and
11.35
,
A_{3}=\emptyset
.
By Lemma
11.36
,
Y=Y^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Then each vertex in
A_{5}
is universal in
G
by Lemma
11.7
. So
A_{5}=\emptyset
by Lemma
2.11
.
Since
q_{i-2}
is not a small vertex,
|B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. By symmetry,
|B_{i-2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. It follows from Lemma
11.18
that
A_{1}(i-1)
and
A_{1}(i+1)
are cliques.

Suppose that
X
contains a vertex
a
and
X^{\prime}
contains a vertex
b
.
Let
c\in A_{1}(i+1)
be a neighbor of
a
and
d\in A_{1}(i-1)
be a neighbor of
b
. Then
ac
and
bd
contradict Lemma
11.10
.
So we may assume that
X=\emptyset
. Equivalently, each vertex in
A_{2}(i-2)
has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i-1)
. By Lemma
6.2
,
A_{2}(i-2)
is complete to
A_{1}(i-1)
.

Let
v\in A_{2}(i-2)
be a vertex such that
|N(v)\cap A_{1}(i+1)|
is maximum. If
A^{1}_{2}(i+1)
contains a vertex
u
, we show that
\{v,u,q_{i}\}
is a
(1,1)
-good subgraph. It suffices to show that every maximum clique
M
of
G
contains a vertex in
\{v,u,q_{i}\}
. Since
v
is universal in
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
and
u
is universal in
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{1}(i+1)\cup A_{2}(i+1)
,
M
contains
v
or
u
if
M\subseteq B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{1}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i-2)
or
M\subseteq B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{1}(i+1)\cup A_{2}(i+1)
. For the same reason,
M
contains
q_{i}
if
M\subseteq B_{i}\cup B_{j}
for
j\in\{i-1,i+1\}
. The remaining cases are the following.

\bullet

M\subseteq B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}
. Then
|B_{i-2}|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
or
|B_{i+2}|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
. If
|B_{i-2}|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
, then
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
. If
|B_{i+2}|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
, then
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}(i+1)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
.

\bullet

M\subseteq A_{2}(i-2)\cup A_{1}(i+1)
. Then
v\in M
by the choice of
v
.

\bullet

M\subseteq A_{2}(i+1)\cup A_{1}(i-1)
. Then
|A_{2}(i+1)|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
or
|A_{1}(i-1)|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
. If
|A_{2}(i+1)|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
, then
B_{i+2}\cup B_{i+1}\cup A_{2}(i+1)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
. If
|A_{1}(i-1)|\geq\frac{\omega}{2}
, then
A_{1}(i-1)\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
is a clique of size larger than
\omega
.

So
A_{2}^{1}(i+1)=\emptyset
and thus
A_{1}(i+1)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+1)
.

Next we give a
\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil
-coloring of
G
.
Let
x=|B_{i-1}|
,
y=|B_{i-2}|
, and
z=|A_{2}(i-2)|
.
Our strategy is to use
p=x+y+z-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
colors to color some vertices of
G
first and then argue that the remaining vertices can be colored with
f=\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil-p
colors.
Note that

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{5}{4}\omega\right\rceil-(x+y+z-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil)

\displaystyle=\omega+(4\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil-(x+y+z))

\displaystyle\geq\omega,

since
x+y+z\leq\omega
.

Step 1: Precolor a subgraph.

Next we define vertices to be colored with
p
colors. Let
S_{i-1}\subseteq B_{i-1}
be the set of
x-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices,
S_{i-2}\subseteq B_{i-2}
be the set of
y-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices,
S_{z}\subseteq A_{2}(i-2)
be the set of
z-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
vertices, and
S_{i+1}\subseteq B_{i+1}
be the set of
p
vertices with largest neighborhoods in
A_{1}(i+1)
. Since
(x+y+z)-3\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil<|B_{i+1}|
, the choice of
S_{i+1}
is possible.
Since
A_{1}(i+1)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i+1)
, every vertex in
A_{1}(i+1)
has at least
\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil+1
neighbors in
B_{i+1}
and so
S_{i+1}
is complete to
A_{1}(i+1)
.
We color
S_{i+1}
with all colors in
[p]
, and color all vertices in
S_{i-1}\cup S_{i-2}\cup S_{z}
with all colors in
[p]
. Since
B_{i+1}
is anticomplete to
B_{i-1}\cup B_{i-2}\cup A_{2}(i-2)
, this coloring is proper. So it suffices to show that

G_{1}=G-(S_{i-1}\cup S_{i-2}\cup S_{i+1}\cup S_{z})
is
f
-colorable.

Step 2: Reduce by degeneracy.

Let
v
be a vertex in
B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1)
.
Note that
|N_{G_{1}}(v)|<\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil+(\omega-\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil)\leq f
.
It follows that
G_{1}
is
f
-colorable if and only if
G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1))
is
f
-colorable.
Since
G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1))
is chordal,
\chi(G_{1}-(B_{i}\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{1}(i+1)))\leq\omega\leq f
.
∎

11.2
One non-empty
A_{1}(i)

The global assumption in this subsection is that
A_{1}=A_{1}(i)\neq\emptyset
.
Suppose that
x\in A_{1}(i)
. By Proposition
6.1
, the component of
A_{1}(i)
containing
x
has a neighbor
y\in A_{2}(i+1)
. We may assume that
xy\in E(G)
by renaming the vertex.

Lemma 11.40
.

B_{i-2}
and
B_{i+2}
are complete.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.9
,
y
is complete to
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}
. We are done by Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Lemma 11.41
.

A_{2}(i-2)=\emptyset
. By symmetry,
A_{2}(i+1)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
A_{2}(i-2)\neq\emptyset
. Let
K
be a component of
A_{2}(i-2)
. By Lemma
4.15
(2) and
A_{1}=A_{1}(i)
,
K
has no neighbor in
A_{1}
. By Lemma
4.8
,
N(K)\cap(H\cup A_{5})
is a clique.
Since
G
has no clique cutsets,
K
has a neighbor in
A_{3}
.
By Lemmas
7.1
and
4.18
,
N(K)\cap A_{3}\subseteq A_{3}(i-2)
or
N(K)\cap A_{3}\subseteq A_{3}(i-1)
.

Suppose first that
N(K)\cap A_{3}\subseteq A_{3}(i-2)
. Then
N(K)\subseteq B_{i-2}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{5}
. Each vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{5}
is universal in
N(K)
.
By Lemma
4.8
,
N(K)\cap B_{i-1}
and
N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
are complete. Since
G
has no clique cutsets, there exist
a,b\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
7.1
,
a,b
have a common neighbor in
B_{i-1}
and so have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+2}
. Then
a-K-b-(N(b)\cap B_{i+2})-B_{i+1}-(N(x)\cap B_{i})-x
is an induced sequence, a contradiction.
The argument for the case
N(K)\cap A_{3}\subseteq A_{3}(i-1)
is symmetric.
∎

Lemma 11.42
.

A_{3}(i)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
t\in A_{3}(i)
and
b\in B_{i}
be a non-neighbor of
t
. By Lemma
4.12
, we may assume that
b
and
t
have a common neighbor
c\in B_{i-1}
and have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i+1}
. Since
t-c-b-(N(b)\cap B_{i+1})-q_{i+2}-y-x
is not a bad
P_{7}
,
bx\in E(G)
. By symmetry,
tx\in E(G)
. But this contradicts Lemma
11.5
.
∎

Let
S=A_{2}(i-1)\cap N(A_{1}(i))
.

Lemma 11.43
.

For any vertex in
S
, it has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i)\cap N(A_{2}(i+2))
.

Proof.

Let
s\in S
and
a\in A_{1}(i)
be a neighbor of
s
. Suppose by contradiction that
a\notin A_{1}(i)\cap N(A_{2}(i+2))
. Let
b\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
s
. By Lemma
11.6
,
a
has a neighbor
a^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i)\cap N(A_{2}(i+2))
. Since
sa^{\prime}\notin E(G)
,
b-s-a-a^{\prime}-(N(a^{\prime})\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.44
.

For any component
K
of
A_{2}(i-1)\setminus S
,
K
has two non-adjacent neighbors in
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
complete to
K
.

Proof.

Note that
N(K)\subseteq S\cup A_{5}\cup B_{i}\cup A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
by Lemma
11.42
. We first show that each vertex in
N(K)\cap S
is universal in
N(K)
.
Suppose by contradiction that
s\in N(K)\cap S
is not adjacent to
t\in N(K)
.
By Lemma
11.43
, let
s^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i)\cap N(A_{2}(i+2))
be a neighbor of
s
.
If
s
mixes on
ab\in K
, then
a-b-s-s^{\prime}-(N(s^{\prime})\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
.
So
s
is complete to
K
and thus

s
and
t
have a common neighbor
u\in K
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
ts^{\prime}\notin E(G)
and thus
t\notin A_{5}
. So
t\in S\cup B_{i}\cup B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-1)
and then
t-u-s-s^{\prime}-(N(s^{\prime})\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is a bad
P_{7}
. This shows that each vertex in
N(K)\cap S
is universal in
N(K)
.
This implies that each vertex in
N(K)\cap A_{5}
is universal in
N(K)
. If
s\in N(K)\cap B_{i}
is not adjacent to
t\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
, then
t-K-s-q_{i+1}-q_{i+2}-(N(t)\cap B_{i-2})-t
is an induced cyclic sequence. So each vertex in
N(K)\cap B_{i}
is universal in
N(K)
. Therefore, each vertex in
N(K)\setminus A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
is universal in
N(K)
.

By Lemma
7.2
, each pair of non-adjacent vertices in
N(K)\setminus A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
has a common neighbor in
K
and so has disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-2}
. If
K\cup A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
contains an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
d\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
and
c\in K
and
a,b\in(N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1))\cup K
, then
a-b-c-d-(N(d)\cap B_{i-2})-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
K\cup A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
contains no such induced
P_{4}
s.

Therefore, all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K
where
X=A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
and
Y=B_{i-2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 11.45
.

One of
A_{2}(i-1)
and
A_{2}(i)
is empty.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
v\in A_{2}(i-1)
and
w\in A_{2}(i)
.
By Lemma
6.4
(1),
A_{1}(i)
is anticomplete to
A_{2}(i)\cup A_{2}(i-1)
.
By Lemma
11.44
,
v
has two non-adjacent neighbors
a,b
in
B_{i-1}\cup A_{3}(i-1)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
a,b
do not have a common neighbor in
B_{i-2}
. Then
a-v-b-(N(b)\cap B_{i-2})-q_{i+2}-(N(w)\cap B_{i+1})-w
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.46
.

Either
A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
or
A_{3}(i-2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
s\in A_{3}(i-2)
and
t\in A_{3}(i+2)
. Let
a\in B_{i-2}
be a non-neighbor of
s
and
b\in B_{i-2}
be a non-neighbor of
t
.
By Lemmas
4.12
and
11.40
, there exist vertices
c,d\in B_{i-1}
such that
a-c-d-s
is an induced
P_{4}
and there exist vertices
p,q\in B_{i+1}
such that
b-p-q-t
is an induced
P_{4}
. Since
t-q-p-b-a-c-d
is not an induced
P_{7}
,
at\in E(G)
. Then
s-d-c-a-t-q-p
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Let
T=N(A_{1}(i))\cap A_{2}(i+2)
and
T_{0}=A_{2}(i+2)\setminus T
.

Lemma 11.47
.

The following holds for
A_{3}(i-2)
.

(1)

A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
T
.

(2)

A_{3}(i-2)
is complete to
B_{i+2}
.

This implies by
C_{4}
-freeness that
T
or
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
is a clique.

Proof.

We prove one by one.

(1) Let
t\in A_{3}(i-2)
be non-adjacent to
y^{\prime}\in T
. By definition,
y^{\prime}
has a neighbor
x^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i)
.
Since
B_{i-2}
is complete to
B_{i+2}
by Lemma
11.40
,
t
has a non-neighbor in
B_{i-1}
by Lemma
4.12
and so
t
mixes on an edge
ab\in B_{i-1}
. Then
a-b-t-(N(t)\cap B_{i+2})-y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}-(N(x^{\prime})\cap B_{i})
is a bad
P_{7}
.

(2) This follows from (1) and Lemma
4.8
.
∎

Next we explore the internal structure of
A_{2}(i+2)
.

Lemma 11.48
.

There exists
j\in\{i-2,i+2\}
such that
every component of
T_{0}
has two non-adjacent neighbors in either
T
or
A^{\prime}_{3}(j)
but not in both and it is complete to two non-adjacent vertices in
T
or
A^{\prime}_{3}(j)
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a component of
T_{0}
. By symmetry, we may assume that
A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
by Lemma
11.46
.
Note that
N(K)\subseteq A_{3}(i-2)\cup B_{i-2}\cup T\cup B_{i+2}\cup A_{5}
.
By Lemma
11.47
, each vertex in
N(K)\cap(A_{5}\cup B_{i+2})
is universal in
N(K)
, and either each vertex in
N(K)\cap T
or each vertex in
N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
is universal in
N(K)
.

Suppose first that
T
is a clique and so each vertex in
N(K)\setminus A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
is universal in
N(K)
. By Lemma
11.47
(2), each pair of non-adjacent vertices in
N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
has disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-1}
.
If there is an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
d\in N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
,
c\in K
and
a,b\in(N(K)\cap A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1))\cup K
, then
a-b-c-d-(N(d)\cap B_{i-1})-q_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction. So all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K
where
X=A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
and
Y=B_{i-1}
. Therefore, the lemma follows.

Now suppose that
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
is a clique. Then each vertex in
N(K)\setminus T
is universal in
N(K)
. For any pair of non-adjacent vertices in
N(K)\cap T
, they have a common neighbor
q_{i+2}
and so have disjoint complete neighborhoods in
A_{1}(i)
by
(C_{4},C_{6},C_{7})
-freeness of
G
.
If there is an induced
P_{4}=a-b-c-d
with
d\in N(K)\cap T
,
c\in K
and
a,b\in(N(K)\cap T)\cup K
, then
a-b-c-d-(N(d)\cap A_{1}(i))-q_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
, a contradiction. So all three conditions of Lemma
10.1
are satisfied for
K
where
X=T
and
Y=A_{1}(i)
. Therefore, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 11.49
.

Each vertex in
A_{5}
is a universal vertex in
G
.

Proof.

We first show that
A_{5}
is complete to
A_{2}(i+2)
.
By the choice of
C
,
A_{5}
is complete to
T
. By Lemma
11.48
, every component
K
of
T_{0}
has two non-adjacent vertices
\{a,b\}
complete to
K
where
a,b\in T
or
a,b\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
. Since
A_{5}
is complete to
T\cup A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
,
A_{5}
is complete to
K
by
C_{4}
-free.

Next we show that
A_{5}
is complete to
A_{2}(i)\cup A_{2}(i-1)
. Suppose first that
s\in A_{2}(i-1)
has no neighbor in
A_{1}(i)
. By Lemma
11.44
,
s
has two non-adjacent vertices
\{a,b\}
with
a,b\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
. Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
A_{5}
is complete to
s
. Now suppose that
s
has a neighbor in
A_{1}(i)
. By Lemma
11.43
,
s
has a neighbor
s^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i)
which has a neighbor in
A_{2}(i+2)
. Then
A_{5}
is complete to
s^{\prime}
. Since
s-(N(s)\cap B_{i-1})-A_{5}-s^{\prime}-s
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
A_{5}
is complete to
s
. This proves that
A_{5}
is complete to
A_{2}(i-1)\cup A_{2}(i)
.

The lemma now follows from Lemma
11.7
.
∎

So we may assume that
A_{5}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 11.50
.

There exists a vertex
v\in B_{i-1}\setminus N(A_{3}(i-1))
such that
|N(v)\cap B_{i-2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
. By symmetry, There exists a vertex
u\in B_{i+1}\setminus N(A_{3}(i+1))
such that
|N(u)\cap B_{i+2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

If
A_{3}(i-1)\neq\emptyset
, let
v
be a vertex in
B_{i-1}\setminus N(A_{3}(i-1))
with minimal neighborhood in
B_{i}
.
If
A_{3}(i-1)=\emptyset
, then let
v=q_{i-1}
if
B_{i-1}
is complete to
B_{i}
, and let
v\in B_{i-1}
be a vertex with minimal neighborhood in
B_{i}
if
B_{i-1}
is not complete to
B_{i}
. We show that
v
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)
and
N(v)\cap(B_{i-1}\cup B_{i})
is a clique. Suppose first that
A_{3}(i-1)\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
8.11
,
v
is anticomplete to
A_{3}(i-2)
.
By Lemma
8.1
(3) and the choice of
v
,
N(v)\cap(B_{i-1}\cup B_{i})
is a clique. If
v=q_{i-1}
, then the claim holds by the choice of
v
.
If
A_{3}(i-1)=\emptyset
and
B_{i-1}
is not complete to
B_{i}
, then the claim holds by Lemma
8.10
and the choice of
v
.
So

N(v)\setminus B_{i-2}\subseteq B_{i-1}\cup B_{i}\cup S\cup(A_{2}(i-1)\setminus S)
.
Suppose that
N(v)\setminus B_{i-2}
contains two non-adjacent vertices
s
and
s^{\prime}
. Suppose first that
s\in S
. By Lemma
11.43
, we can choose
p\in A_{1}(i)\cap N(A_{2}(i+2))
to be a neighbor of
s
. Since
s^{\prime}-v-s-p-s^{\prime}
is not an induced
C_{4}
,
ps^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. Then
s^{\prime}-v-s-p-(N(p)\cap A_{2}(i+2))-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is a bad
P_{7}
. So
s\notin S
. Since
N(v)\cap(B_{i-1}\cup B_{i})
is a clique, we may assume that
s\in A_{2}(i-1)\setminus S
. By Lemma
11.44
,
s
has two non-adjacent neighbors
a,b
in
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-1)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
a\in A_{3}(i-1)
. Since
s
is a common neighbor of
a,b
, we have
a,b
have disjoint neighborhoods in
B_{i-2}
. Since
b-s-a-N(a)\cap B_{i-2}-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}-B_{i}
is not a bad
P_{7}
, we have
a
is complete to
B_{i}
. It follows that
a,s^{\prime}
and
a,v
have a common neighbor in
B_{i}
and so
N(a)\cap B_{i-2}
is anticomplete to
s^{\prime}
and
v
. Since
va\notin E(G)
by the choice of
v
,
s^{\prime}-v-s-a
is an induced
P_{4}
. Then
s^{\prime}-v-s-a-(N(a)\cap B_{i-2})-q_{i+2}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
or
C_{7}
.
So
N(v)\setminus B_{i-2}
is a clique.
Since
G
has no small vertices,
|N(v)\cap B_{i-2}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.
∎

Lemma 11.51
.

T_{0}
is a clique.

Proof.

Suppose not. Let
K
and
K^{\prime}
be two components of
T_{0}
. Let
v\in K
and
v^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
. By Lemma
11.48
,
K
has two non-adjacent neighbors
a,b
complete to
K
and
K^{\prime}
has two non-adjacent neighbors
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
complete to
K^{\prime}
.

Suppose first that
a,b\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
. Then
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
.
Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
v^{\prime}
is not adjacent to either
a
or
b
. If
v^{\prime}a\in E(G)
, then
v^{\prime}-a-v-b-(N(b)\cap B_{i-1})-q_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
v^{\prime}a,v^{\prime}b\notin E(G)
. By symmetry,
va^{\prime},vb^{\prime}\notin E(G)
. So
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}
are pairwise distinct. If
aa^{\prime}\in E(G)
, then
a^{\prime}b\notin E(G)
or
a^{\prime}-a-v-b-a^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
, and so
a^{\prime}-a-v-b-N_{B_{i-1}}(b)-q_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
\{a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\}
is a stable set. Let
c,c^{\prime}\in B_{i-1}
be a neighbor of
a,a^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
b-v-a-c-c^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-v^{\prime}
is an induced
P_{7}
.

Suppose now that
a,b\in T
. Then
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in T
.
Since
G
is
C_{4}
-free,
v^{\prime}
is not adjacent to either
a
or
b
. If
v^{\prime}a\in E(G)
, then
v^{\prime}-a-v-b-(N(b)\cap A_{1}(i))-B_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
v^{\prime}a,v^{\prime}b\notin E(G)
. By symmetry,
va^{\prime},vb^{\prime}\notin E(G)
.
So
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}
are pairwise distinct. If
aa^{\prime}\in E(G)
, then
a^{\prime}b\notin E(G)
or
a^{\prime}-a-v-b-a^{\prime}
is an induced
C_{4}
, and so
a^{\prime}-a-v-b-N_{A_{1}(i)}(b)-B_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced sequence. So
\{a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\}
is a stable set. Let
c,c^{\prime}\in A_{1}(i)
be a neighbor of
a,a^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
b-v-a-c-c^{\prime}-a^{\prime}-v^{\prime}
is an induced
P_{7}
.
∎

Lemma 11.52
.

Either
T
is a clique or
A_{3}(i-2)\cup A_{3}(i+2)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose that
T
contains two non-adjacent vertices
a
and
b
. If
t\in A_{3}(i-2)
, then
t
has a non-neighbor
s\in B_{i-2}
and it follows from Lemma
11.47
that
a-s-b-t-a
is an induced
C_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 11.53
.

For any non-empty component
K
of
A_{3}(i-2)
and any simplicial vertex
t\in K
,
N(t)\setminus B_{i-1}
is a clique and so
|N(t)\cap B_{i-1}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Note that
N(t)\setminus B_{i-1}\subseteq K\cup B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}\cup T\cup T_{0}
. Suppose that
N(t)\setminus B_{i-1}
contains two non-adjacent vertices
v
and
u
. Suppose first that
v,u\notin T_{0}
. Since
B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}\cup T
is a clique by Lemma
11.52

and
N_{K}(t)
is a clique, we may assume that
v\in K
and
u\in B_{i-2}\cup B_{i+2}\cup T
. Since
B_{i+2}\cup T
is complete to
A_{3}(i-2)
by Lemma
11.47
,
u\in B_{i-2}
. But this contradicts Lemma
8.3
. So we may assume that
v\in T_{0}
. By Lemma
11.48
,
v
has two non-adjacent neighbors
a,b\in T
or
A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
. By Lemma
11.52
,
a,b\in A^{\prime}_{3}(i-2)
. We may assume that
a\in A_{3}(i-2)
and
t\neq b
. If
tb\notin E(G)
, then
u-t-v-b-(N(b)\cap B_{i-1})-q_{i}-q_{i+1}
is an induced
P_{7}
. So
tb\in E(G)
and so
t\neq a
. By symmetry,
ta\in E(G)
. Since
t
is simplicial in
K
,
b\in B_{i-2}
and so
b
mixes on
at\in E(K)
. This contradicts Lemma
8.3
.
∎

Lemma 11.54
.

|B_{i}|>\left\lceil\frac{\omega}{4}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.45
, we may assume that
A_{2}(i-1)=\emptyset
.
We choose a vertex
v\in B_{i-1}
anticomplete to
